The bound is junction's `http.maxBodySize`, the one a browser's multipart upload already meets, so it is not a new option. Its default is 256KB and base64 adds a third, so about 190KB of file gets through untouched: a phone photo (2–5MB) is refused until the app raises that setting, which is the same raise a form upload of that photo already needs. Today the 413 reads *Request body exceeds 262144 bytes* and names nothing to raise, so it names `http.maxBodySize`. One call, and the server stays the only author of a ref.** Probed 2026-10-08 (FJS-2062): with FileStorage `local` installed, a principal's `photo.create({ data: { original: <FileRef object> } })` is refused by `refuseUnbindable` with the atomic-operator message, because an object that is not bytes is not `isFileValue`; a `data:image/png;base64,…` string is refused by `readValue` as *a string is neither*; and `generateJsonSchema` gives the column `$ref: FileRef`, the STORED shape (`key`, `bucket`, `provider`, `size`, `mime`, `uploadedAt`), as the tool's input. Over JSON nothing reaches a File column, so no upload flow runs through an app's own agent.
  - **A** — an inline value: a data URL (RFC 2397, `data:<mime>;base64,…`) or `{ name, type, data }` with base64 `data`, which `readValue` decodes into bytes like a Buffer, `@accept` sniffing the bytes and not trusting the claimed type. The input schema for a File column is derived to describe that value rather than `FileRef`. The bound is junction's `http.maxBodySize`, the one a browser's multipart upload already meets, so it is not a new option. Its default is 256KB and base64 adds a third, so about 190KB of file gets through untouched: a phone photo (2–5MB) is refused until the app raises that setting, which is the same raise a form upload of that photo already needs. Today the 413 reads *Request body exceeds 262144 bytes* and names nothing to raise, so it names `http.maxBodySize`. One call, and the server stays the only author of a ref.
  - **B** — an upload route that takes the bytes and hands back a ref the create then accepts as an object. That needs the ref signed or checked against what the route issued: a ref the caller made up and naming another row's key would have that file deleted when this row's old refs are cleaned up. It is two steps per transport, and it is the PUT presign FJS-D260 already says comes later for direct-to-bucket upload.
  - **C** — both, A now and B when direct-to-bucket lands.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the problem's own complexity (JSON has no multipart), it uses an existing name, its owner is the `readValue` already there, and B's forged-ref failure is destructive and silent unless it brings a signing scheme that is out of scale for this row. When B arrives, it arrives as FJS-D260's presign and does not depend on this.

- ~~**FJS-D667 — How does the compiler tell a resource handle's destructure from a meant snapshot?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D667`): A — a runtime check: the destructure expansion, in dev builds only, reads the source's property descriptors and warns once when a destructured key is an accessor. The shape is a fact at runtime, so nothing is declared and a meant snapshot over a plain object stays quiet.** `const { rows, loading } = r.list()` compiles with no warning (probed 2026-10-08: `analysis.warnings` is `[]`) and emits `const rows = r.list().rows`, read once at setup, though the handle's getters read signals. FJS-1969 made destructuring at the call the quiet spelling for a snapshot meant on purpose (`const { port } = load()`, pinned by `const-promotion.test.js` "is quiet for a destructure at the call"), and the compiler cannot see the callee's shape. Red test: a `<script>` with `import { r } from './r.js'; const { rows, loading } = r.list()` should carry a `read once` warning, while `const { port: out } = load()` stays quiet.
  - **A** — a runtime check: the destructure expansion, in dev builds only, reads the source's property descriptors and warns once when a destructured key is an accessor. The shape is a fact at runtime, so nothing is declared and a meant snapshot over a plain object stays quiet.
  - **B** — the handle declares itself: `createResource`'s handle carries a marker (a symbol), and the same runtime check keys on it. Cheaper than A but a hand-rolled handle is not covered.
  - **C** — warn on every top-level destructure of a call result and give a meant snapshot another spelling (`untrack(() => …)` or `snapshot()`); this retires FJS-1969's quiet spelling and adds a noun.
  - **Recommend A** — the compiler lacks the shape and the runtime has it, which is derive-instead-of-declare; it adds no noun and no option, and a wrong destructure becomes a dev-time warning rather than silence. Under every option the expansion must evaluate the call ONCE: probed 2026-10-09, `const { rows, loading } = r.list()` emits `const rows = r.list().rows; const loading = r.list().loading` and `const { port, host } = load()` calls `load()` twice, so `loading` reads a second handle (a second fetch) and not `rows`'s. A's check reads its descriptors off the one temporary that fix introduces.

- **FJS-D668 — Should a named slot block (`slot="header"`) satisfy a component's snippet prop of the same name?** `<Card><h2 slot="header">T</h2></Card>` drops `T` today (probed 2026-10-08 with `renderComponent`: output is `<article class="card"><p>BODY</p></article>`). It does WARN — `FJS-926`'s `warnUnrenderableSlots` names `<Card>` and the slot — so the row's "no warning" is stale; what is left is that the content is dropped. The kit has 20+ snippet props (Card header/footer, Modal/Drawer footer, Table actions/empty/footer, SectionHeader/EmptyState action, Popover trigger/content, Button leading/trailing, Input icon, StatCard body…); only `Form.actions` honours both spellings, by hand. The red test: `<Card><h2 slot="header">HEAD</h2></Card>` renders `HEAD` inside `.surface-header`.
  - **A** — the `$slots` fallback Form has, copied into every component with a snippet prop. Each copy is a restatement of one rule, ~20 sites, and a new component forgets it.
  - **B** — Mesa owns the translation: at construction, a block key that is not a declared slot but IS a declared prop of that name is delivered as the snippet prop (in `makeSlots`/`warnUnrenderableSlots`'s neighborhood; the compiler hands `makeSlots` the declared SLOT names today and would hand it the declared props too, the list `restProps` already receives). Then `Form`'s hand fallback is deleted and the warning keeps firing only for a name that is neither. Touches Mesa runtime semantics, so it wants a mesa test and the `ui` suite.
  - **C** — warn only: keep `FJS-926` and sharpen its text to say "this component takes `header` as a snippet prop: `{#snippet header()}…{/snippet}`"; `slot=` stays a different protocol. No behavior change; Form's fallback stays the one exception.
  - **Recommend B** — one owner for a two-protocol translation (Invariant 4's reasoning), it makes the kit uniform so knowing one component teaches the next, and it deletes Form's special case rather than adding 20 more. It also closes `FJS-1230`, the same defect filed 2026-09-21 and still open.

- ~~**FJS-D669 — How does a picker learn which related rows a create policy will accept, and does `fli advise` name the case the schema cannot carry?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D669`): C — both: A for the one-hop `==` auth() shape the derivation can state, B's advise rule for every create conjunct it cannot (`||`, `in`, a computed claim).** `determinedColumns` (`packages/litestone/src/core/policy.js:903`) only recognizes `<scalar> == auth().<claim>`; a create policy `@@allow('create', schedule.hostId == auth().id)` has no emission, so `x-litestone-policies` stays `true` and `resource.options('scheduleId')` (`packages/sierra/src/resource/resource.js:2373`) lists every readable `Schedule`, of which the write refuses all but the caller's. `FJS-D492` took A (derive `x-determined`) over B (an `fli advise` rule); the relational conjunct is the case A did not reach, and no advise rule exists for it either.
  - **A** — extend the derivation: a top-level create conjunct of the shape `<relation>.<col> == auth().<claim>` emits `x-pick-where: { scheduleId: { hostId: 'auth().id' } }`, sierra's `options()` merges it into the related query as a filter with the claim filled from `session.user`, and the server still refuses (Invariant 6). One new schema keyword beside `x-determined`.
  - **B** — no new keyword: the related model's own READ policy is the owner of "rows this caller may pick", and `fli advise` names the model pair where a create policy reaches through a relation the related model's read gate does not narrow, so the author narrows the read or writes a `where` on the field's `optionsQuery`.
  - **C** — both: A for the one-hop `==` auth() shape the derivation can state, B's advise rule for every create conjunct it cannot (`||`, `in`, a computed claim).
  - **Recommend C** — A alone leaves every unpinnable policy silent, B alone makes every author hand-write what a `@@allow` already says; the derivable shape is derived (`FJS-D492`'s reasoning) and the rest is named rather than guessed.

- ~~**FJS-D670 — How does Basecamp turn on OAuth when auth requires `cookieAuth` and the whole app is Bearer?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D670`): B — a switch, as `example/api/src/app.ts` does with `SHOP_COOKIE_AUTH` (`COOKIE_AUTH` gates both `cookieAuth` and the `oauth` block, ~85/447/472): cookie mode and `oauth` turn on together exactly when at least one provider has its env vars set, and stay Bearer otherwise. The web client's `cookieAuth` twin (`FJS-787`) must follow the same switch, and the manifest carries no auth fact today, so either it gains one or the web build reads the same env. A fleet that wants SSO gets it; a fleet that sets a provider loses the desktop shell's login, as under A. Two modes to test.** `createAuthPlugin` throws at boot on `{ oauth }` without `{ cookieAuth: true }` (`packages/auth/plugin.ts:126`: the callback is a browser redirect and can only hand back a cookie). Basecamp's `app.ts` passes neither, so `GET /auth/oauth` answers `{ providers: [] }` (`plugin.ts:429`). The red test: mount basecamp with a provider configured and assert `GET /auth/oauth` lists it and `GET /auth/oauth/{provider}` redirects; today it lists nothing. Cookie mode also needs the twin `junction.cookieAuth` in the web client's sierra config (`FJS-787`), and Basecamp's desktop shell serves the page from `tauri://localhost`, where a cookie set by the API origin does not ride (`web/src/session.js` already special-cases the client origin for this).
  - **A** — move the whole app onto cookie sessions: `cookieAuth: true` plus the sierra twin, providers built from env (`GITHUB_*`, `GOOGLE_*`, `OIDC_*`), login screen draws one button per `GET /auth/oauth` entry. One mode. The server still ACCEPTS `Authorization: Bearer` and `x-api-key` under cookie mode (`http.ts` `setAuthCookie`), so API keys keep working; what changes is every LOGIN, whose token now goes into a cookie and leaves the body (`plugin.ts` ~697), so the desktop shell on `tauri://localhost` and any password-login script can no longer sign in, and every Basecamp drive re-tests under a different credential.
  - **B** — a switch, as `example/api/src/app.ts` does with `SHOP_COOKIE_AUTH` (`COOKIE_AUTH` gates both `cookieAuth` and the `oauth` block, ~85/447/472): cookie mode and `oauth` turn on together exactly when at least one provider has its env vars set, and stay Bearer otherwise. The web client's `cookieAuth` twin (`FJS-787`) must follow the same switch, and the manifest carries no auth fact today, so either it gains one or the web build reads the same env. A fleet that wants SSO gets it; a fleet that sets a provider loses the desktop shell's login, as under A. Two modes to test.
  - **C** — wait for auth to build the short-lived-code exchange the `plugin.ts:117` comment names as the missing piece, so a Bearer app can finish a redirect flow; Basecamp then passes `oauth` with no mode change. Nothing ships until auth does.
  - **Recommend B** — `example/` already proves the switch (`verify:oauth`), it adds no noun, and a fleet with no provider configured keeps every login it has today; A breaks the shell's login for every fleet and C blocks a table-stakes feature on a larger build. C stays the real fix for the shell and is owed to auth either way.

- ~~**FJS-D671 — Where does a list's selection live: a standalone set primitive the screen composes, a Table-owned column, or a resource-owned set?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-12 (`FJS-D671`): A — a standalone signal-backed primitive in `@frontierjs/ui` (a `.js` module beside `dnd.js`): `createSelection(keyOf)` with `has(row)`, `toggle(row)`, `clear()`, `set(rows)`, `count`, and `stateOf(rows)` returning `none | some | all`; plus `Checkbox` `indeterminate` and a `<BulkBar {selection}>` that shows the count and a slot for the actions. The screen draws the header box through `header(col)` and the row box in its own snippet. No change to `Table`.** `FJS-2003`: a screen that multi-selects hand-rolls a `Set`, a select-all `<input>` with `bind:this` and an effect setting `.indeterminate` (`Checkbox.mesa` has no such prop), and the bulk bar. `Table.mesa` cannot draw the per-row box itself, because the caller's `row` snippet supplies its own `<tr>`; `rowKey` (`FJS-1622`) and `header(col)` (`FJS-1625`) are the identity and the slot, and what is missing is the set between them. `Checkbox` `indeterminate` is owed under every option.
  - **A** — a standalone signal-backed primitive in `@frontierjs/ui` (a `.js` module beside `dnd.js`): `createSelection(keyOf)` with `has(row)`, `toggle(row)`, `clear()`, `set(rows)`, `count`, and `stateOf(rows)` returning `none | some | all`; plus `Checkbox` `indeterminate` and a `<BulkBar {selection}>` that shows the count and a slot for the actions. The screen draws the header box through `header(col)` and the row box in its own snippet. No change to `Table`.
  - **B** — `Table` owns it: `selectable` and `bind:selected` draw a leading select column, the header tri-state box, and hand the row snippet a `selected` flag. Fewest lines in a screen, but the table must draw a cell inside a `<tr>` the caller wrote, which breaks the contract `row` has today.
  - **C** — the resource owns it: `resource.selection` in sierra's `createResource`, so the set survives paging, filtering and live-store updates and names ids rather than rows. Right for ELA's inbox, but it makes UI-kit selection depend on sierra, and a list not backed by a resource has nowhere to put one.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the smallest piece that deletes the hand-rolled Set, bar and `.indeterminate` effect, it adds no coupling, and C can wrap it later (a resource handing out a `createSelection` keyed by primary key) without changing it. The sighting rules C out as the first piece anyway: ELA's inbox selects messages AND tickets in one column (`FJS-2003`), two resources, so a resource-owned set cannot hold that one selection, while `keyOf` can key it by kind and id.

- ~~**FJS-D672 — Who sorts when a `<Table>` has no `onsort`: the Table locally, the caller only, or an explicit opt-in?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D672`): A — the Table sorts locally when no `onsort` is stated: it owns the sort, so `rows` reach the row snippet ordered by `orderBy`. `onsort` stays the "the sort lives elsewhere (URL, server)" switch. A caller that sorts server-side behind `bind:orderBy` is sorted a second time, harmlessly for a stable sort on the same key. The comparator is a fact with one owner, `comparatorFor`/`compareValues` in junction's `query-values.ts`; `@frontierjs/ui` depends on toolbelt only, so that comparator would have to move down to `@frontierjs/toolbelt/query` first. The case it gets wrong in silence is a Table over ONE PAGE of a server list with no `onsort` (basecamp's `apps/index.mesa` and `deployments/index.mesa` pass `rows={list.rows}` and no `onsort`): it sorts the page, not the list. The switch already exists, `onsort={list.sort}` (ELA's `forms`, `actions`, `integrations` screens), so a list handle's Table states it.** `FJS-1975`: `resource.columns()` marks every column `sortable`, and `handleSort` (`packages/ui/components/display/Table.mesa`) with no `onsort` only assigns `orderBy = next`, which moves the arrow and reorders nothing unless the caller bound `orderBy` and re-sorts its own rows. The component cannot tell `bind:orderBy` from a plain `orderBy=` prop. The red test: a Table given `columns` with `sortable: true`, three rows and no `onsort`; click the header; the row order is unchanged today and the assertion is that it changed (or that no button was drawn, under B).
  - **A** — the Table sorts locally when no `onsort` is stated: it owns the sort, so `rows` reach the row snippet ordered by `orderBy`. `onsort` stays the "the sort lives elsewhere (URL, server)" switch. A caller that sorts server-side behind `bind:orderBy` is sorted a second time, harmlessly for a stable sort on the same key. The comparator is a fact with one owner, `comparatorFor`/`compareValues` in junction's `query-values.ts`; `@frontierjs/ui` depends on toolbelt only, so that comparator would have to move down to `@frontierjs/toolbelt/query` first. The case it gets wrong in silence is a Table over ONE PAGE of a server list with no `onsort` (basecamp's `apps/index.mesa` and `deployments/index.mesa` pass `rows={list.rows}` and no `onsort`): it sorts the page, not the list. The switch already exists, `onsort={list.sort}` (ELA's `forms`, `actions`, `integrations` screens), so a list handle's Table states it.
  - **B** — the affordance needs `onsort`: no `onsort`, no button, whatever the column says. `bind:orderBy` retires (the Table never owns the sort, it only states the move), the `orderBy = next` branch is deleted, and the datatable spec's bound-sort cases move to `onsort`. Smallest change, but every local-data table has to hand-write the sort that `columns()` already promised.
  - **C** — an explicit opt-in prop (`sort="local"`) beside `onsort`. Honest, but a new option on a component whose default is the broken case, and `columns()` still marks everything sortable.
  - **Recommend A** — `resource.columns()` already declares what can be sorted, so a Table handed `columns` and `rows` should sort them; it adds no option, and the server-sorted case already has its switch in `onsort`. Move the comparator into toolbelt first so the Table does not import junction.

- ~~**FJS-D673 — Does a plain multi-line String (an address, a note) get a schema word now, or wait for a sighting?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-09 (`FJS-D673`): B — `@syntax(txt)`: plain text's file extension beside `md`, the catalog's own rule for the argument; `x-syntax: txt` → `textarea` with plain display in `displayFor` (no Markdown rendering). No new attribute, and a natural language is a syntax too. It redefines an existing answer rather than adding one: probed 2026-10-09, `controlFor({ type: 'string', 'x-syntax': 'text' })` answers `{ control: 'code', language: 'text' }` today, an unhighlighted code editor that falls through rather than a designed answer.** `FJS-1657`: the code half is answered (`@syntax(lang)` → control `code`; `@syntax(md)` → `textarea`), and what is left is text that is not a syntax. Today `controlFor` (`sierra/src/resource/field-rules.js:692`) answers `textarea` only for `x-syntax: md`, so `example/db/schema.lite` `note String? @length(0, 500)` renders as `<input type=text>` and a pasted newline is stripped. The red test: `controlFor` over `{ type: 'string', maxLength: 500 }` answers `input` today and the assertion is `textarea`. No stressor has hit it; the row itself says "no sighting yet".
  - **A** — wait: close the row's residue until an app declares a column that needs it, then the sighting names the shape. No code, no noun.
  - **B** — `@syntax(txt)`: plain text's file extension beside `md`, the catalog's own rule for the argument; `x-syntax: txt` → `textarea` with plain display in `displayFor` (no Markdown rendering). No new attribute, and a natural language is a syntax too. It redefines an existing answer rather than adding one: probed 2026-10-09, `controlFor({ type: 'string', 'x-syntax': 'text' })` answers `{ control: 'code', language: 'text' }` today, an unhighlighted code editor that falls through rather than a designed answer.
  - **C** — derive from `@length`: a `maxLength` above a threshold (say 200) is `textarea`. No new word, but a magic number, and a column that is long and one-line (a URL) is misdrawn.
  - **Recommend A** — nothing has asked for it, and the three shapes above each cost a different noun; a real sighting (an address field someone cannot fill) will say whether it is B or a layout hint instead. The hand-drawn `<Textarea>`s in the prototypes do not count as one: chatwoot's `CannedResponse.content String @length(1, 8000)` (`web/src/routes/replies/index.mesa`) is a screen written by hand, so no generated form was in its path; the sighting that counts is a generated `<Form>` over a plain String.

- **FJS-D674 — How does a relation picker add what tells repeated labels apart (`New · New · New · New request`): widen `@@label` to a column list, or a runtime `labelField` on `options()` / the Form?** `FJS-2021`: `options('statusId')` maps each row to `row[shown]` where `shown` is ONE column (`labelField ?? related.labelField`, `packages/sierra/src/resource/resource.js`), so ELA's TaskStatus (`new` repeats per desk) answers four `New`s. The red assertion: `options('statusId')` over rows `{id:1,name:'New',desk:'Sales'}` and `{id:2,name:'New',desk:'Support'}` yields two options with distinct labels — today both are `New`. `FJS-D243` already names this: a picker subtitle is the second reader that reopens `@@label([a, b, c])`, "the existing noun widened, never a second beside it".
  - **A** — widen `@@label` to a list of OWN columns, per `FJS-D243`: litestone parses and checks each entry by the rules one entry has today, `x-label-field` carries the list, `labelFieldInfo` returns it, and `options()` joins the non-null parts with ` · ` (a Search matches any of them). One declaration, and every picker, ⌘K and detail line agrees. It solves neither measured case alone: ELA's desk is `TaskStatus.group` → `TaskGroup` via `groupId` (`fjs-prototypes/ela/db/schema.lite`), and a `@generated` column cannot read another table, so the desk name is not an own column there.
  - **B** — the same list, and an entry may be ONE hop through a to-one relation (`@@label([group.name, label])`): the parser checks the hop is to-one and the far column passes the one-entry rules, `options()` asks for the relation's column with the rows, a null relation (ELA's global steps, `groupId` null) drops its part, and a Search on that part is a relation filter. Two hops, a to-many, or a far column the far model's gate hides are refused at parse by name. `FJS-2130` (connectteam, `Employee` → `user.name`, filed 2026-10-09) is the same one hop and closes with it.
  - **C** — `options(field, { labelField })` already exists as a single column; let it also take a function `(row) => string` (or a list of columns), and `<Form>` / `FormField` forward a per-field `labelField` prop. No schema change, but the answer lives in each screen, so the queue, the create form and ⌘K can disagree — the exact drift `labelFieldInfo` exists to prevent.
  - **Recommend B** — `FJS-D243` pre-decided the spelling (the existing noun widened) and the trigger has now come twice; both sightings, ELA's desk and connectteam's employee name, are one hop through a to-one relation, so A is a first cut that fixes neither, and the `@generated` fallback the earlier draft leaned on cannot cross a table. One hop with everything else refused at parse is the smallest rule that covers what was measured.

- ~~**FJS-D679 — Where does an app's backup live and what takes it: an Outpost route that returns the archive to Basecamp, an Outpost route that keeps it on the machine, or a command run in the container?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D679`): A — Outpost route `POST /backup` runs the capture in the container (`docker exec … litestone backup` for a sqlite app, a `tar` of the named volume otherwise) and stays on the machine. Basecamp records a row per app (`Backup` gains an `appId` and loses `@@tenant(none)`, or a sibling `AppBackup` is coined) with `location` a path on that machine. Smallest wire: no bytes cross the command port. A lost machine takes its backups with it, which is the `local` destination's own comment ("a backup of a disk onto the same disk").** `FJS-1782`: `Backup` is `@@tenant(none)` and `backup-run.job.ts` runs `VACUUM INTO` over Basecamp's own sqlite file; Outpost has no backup route and `volumes.service` names none, so a `database` or volume app on a fleet machine has no archive and no restore. Red test would assert: for an `App` placed on a server, a backup request produces a signed Outpost call and a row that names the app. Today no such call exists. `FJS-D500` already parked scheduling on "Basecamp's per-app backups" and `frontier-cloud.md` step 5 says restore is an Outpost command (stop → restore → start).
  - **A** — Outpost route `POST /backup` runs the capture in the container (`docker exec … litestone backup` for a sqlite app, a `tar` of the named volume otherwise) and stays on the machine. Basecamp records a row per app (`Backup` gains an `appId` and loses `@@tenant(none)`, or a sibling `AppBackup` is coined) with `location` a path on that machine. Smallest wire: no bytes cross the command port. A lost machine takes its backups with it, which is the `local` destination's own comment ("a backup of a disk onto the same disk").
  - **B** — the same capture, but Outpost streams or uploads the archive to the `s3` destination `BackupDestination` already names, and returns only the key and size. This is the only form that survives the machine, but it needs an S3 credential on every machine, which `fleet-ingress.md` D6 refused for a DNS token on the same reasoning.
  - **C** — no new route: Basecamp calls the existing `POST /exec` with the app's own backup command (what `fli` runs today). No Outpost change, but the capture is an unsigned-shape shell string built in Basecamp, and a volume app with no tool in its image cannot be backed up.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the smallest piece that gives an app a backup and a restore, `s3` can be added to it later as B's upload step without changing the route, and C leaves volume apps and the restore (stop → restore → start) with no owner. Settle in the same ruling whether `Backup` widens or an `AppBackup` model is coined, since `Backup`'s `@@gate` and `@@tenant(none)` describe the installation.

- **FJS-D682 — How does a Resource say a read never changes and may be held for the tab?** `FJS-1891`: remnant memoizes `verses.service.invoke('passage' | 'chapter', key)` in a module `Map` because the corpus ships with the release and nothing writes it; `list-cache.js` answers only after a failed call and only for an `@@sync` model, and `FJS-D625` sends a cache of server reads to the Resource. The Resource already holds `_read` and `_options` and clears both in `resetResourcesForIdentityChange`, so the held reads must join that epoch. The red test: two `service.invoke('passage', 'Gen.1')` calls hit the transport twice today; declared immutable, once.
  - **A** — a Resource option naming the methods: `createResource('Verse', { once: ['passage', 'chapter'] })`. The held answer is keyed by method, id and query, lives in the Resource beside `_options`, and clears on the identity epoch. No Litestone change; the declaration sits with the call it caches (a custom method, which the schema does not describe).
  - **B** — a Model attribute (`@@static`): the schema says no write path exists, `x-static` reaches the Resource, and every read of that Model is held. Access-style facts live in the schema (Invariant 6), but a custom method's answer is not a row, so the attribute would have to say which methods, and a Litestone noun is spent on a client cache.
  - **C** — a per-call flag, `invoke(name, id, data, query, { once: true })`. Smallest, but it is the module `Map` moved one line, and each call site must remember to say it.
  - **D** — the service declares it where the method is declared: junction's `methods:` list already carries `read: true` per custom method (`FJS-D505`, `packages/junction/src/core/service.ts`), and an `immutable: true` beside it would reach the Resource through the service description, so the fact has its origin with the code that knows the data ships with the release. Costs a transport the Resource does not have today (it learns no per-method metadata from the server) and a key on the method declaration.
  - **Recommend A** — the immutable thing here is a method's answer, which only the Resource's author knows; A puts it where `options()` already caches, behind the same identity epoch, without a schema word. D is the better origin and the larger build: take it when a second client (jetty, mcp) wants the same hold, and delete A's option in the same change so the fact keeps one origin.

- **FJS-D683 — What do `backfill` and `split` look like in the operations document, and where in a created file do they run?** `FJS-1787` has `rename` built (`packages/litestone/src/core/operations.js`, `OPERATIONS = { rename }`) and `FJS-D603` rules the family but not the spellings. The file generator has two slots a rename never needed: a rebuild's `INSERT … SELECT` names only columns the old table shares (`generateRebuildSQL`, `migrate.js` ~1215-1250, an added column is OMITTED so its DEFAULT fills it), so a new `NOT NULL` link has no way to take a value; and `first` runs before everything, so a `split` has no point between the new model's `CREATE TABLE` and the old model's rebuild that drops the column. Red test: answer → emit → create → apply over 3 seeded `Job` rows with a required `companyId` added; today apply fails or the column is NULL, and a `Page.body` moved to `Block` reads 0 of 8 bodies back.
  - **A** — Two entries in the one document, each stating its source: `{ op: 'backfill', model, field, value }` (a constant), `{ op: 'backfill', model, field, first: 'Company' }` (the first parent row; refused when the parent is empty), `{ op: 'backfill', model, field, create: { Company: { name: 'Imported' } } }` (one new parent every row links to); and `{ op: 'split', model: 'Page', column: 'body', into: 'Block', field: 'body', link: 'pageId' }`. Litestone adds two slots to `generateMigrationSQL`: a per-column value expression in the rebuild copy, and a `split` slot after new tables, before rebuilds. The shadow's count check is a `-- check` row count after each. Oracle's answer gains `backfill` on an added link and `moves` on a field. Whole of D603's step 2 and 3 in one ruling.
  - **B** — Same document, built in two passes: `backfill` (constant and `first`) now, `split` and `create` parent after. Oracle's answer refuses a move it cannot carry and asks for `risk: migration` with an `escape`, which the contract already has. Smaller, and the row stays open for the second pass.
  - **C** — Oracle-side only: an answer that adds a required link to an entity the app already holds emits it OPTIONAL (`?`) with a `findings` note, and a move is refused. No litestone change; the person tightens it later. The cost is that a model drops a required link the answer meant, and `emit()` still knows nothing of rows, so it must be told which entities are populated.
  - **Recommend B** — `value` and `first` fix the NOT NULL half-apply in both measured cases with one new slot; `split` needs the second slot and the ordering rule, and is better ruled with the first working in front of it.

- **FJS-D684 — What does `SendResult.id` name: the Message-ID on both mailers, the provider's id on both, or a second field for the one it is not?** `FJS-2125`: `createSmtpMailer.send` answers `messageIdOf(msg).id` (`smtp.ts` ~556, minted on the From's domain when no `Message-ID` header is stated), `createResendMailer.send` answers `result.id` from `POST /emails`, which no header carries. An app threading a reply (`In-Reply-To`) off `send().id` works over SMTP and names nothing over Resend. Red test would assert: with the Resend `fetch` stubbed to answer `{ id: 're_123' }`, the id `send()` answers is the `Message-ID` the request body's `headers` carried; today the body carries none and `id` is `re_123`.
  - **A** — `id` is always the Message-ID. The Resend adapter mints one the way `messageIdOf` does (exported from `smtp.ts`, one owner) and sends it in `headers` unless the message states one. Smallest, `SendResult` keeps its shape, and reply threading works under either adapter. It spends Resend's own id, which a delivery webhook (`email.delivered`, `email.bounced`) names the email by, and it relies on Resend honoring a custom `Message-ID` header, which this row did not probe.
  - **B** — `SendResult` gains `messageId`, answered by both adapters (minted and sent by the Resend adapter as in A); `id` stays each provider's own handle, documented as such. Threading and webhook matching both work. Costs one more field across four `IMail`s (the two here, `createTestMailer` in `packages/testing/src/doubles.ts`, Basecamp's `createConduitMailer`), and an app reading `id` still gets two different things by adapter, now named as such.
  - **C** — change nothing in code: document on `SendResult` that `id` is adapter-defined and an app that threads replies states `Message-ID` in `headers` itself. No new noun, but `IMail` stays an interface whose one answer cannot be used without knowing the implementation.
  - **Recommend B** — `id` as the provider handle is what a webhook and a support lookup need, `messageId` is what a reply needs, and an interface that answers one of them under a name that means the other is the defect; A trades one of the two away.

- ~~**FJS-D685 — Does a write fold the app's global filter and the plugins' read filters?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-10 (`FJS-D685`): A — Fold both scopes into `update`, `updateMany`, `remove`, `removeMany`, `delete` and `deleteMany` (move them from `WALKS` to `NOT_RESTORE`). The filter is then one predicate for every verb that reaches a row, matching "every path that reaches a row applies every rule that guards it". The six cells promote to `on`; an update/delete of a hidden row answers `null`/`count: 0` like an absent id. `asSystem()` still does not lift it, so a job that maintains rows the app filter hides must say so with a flag.** `READ_RULES` (`packages/litestone/src/core/client.js` ~4620) places `globalFilter` and `pluginFilters` on `WALKS` only, "narrow a READ and never a write". With `filters: { doc: { status: 'live' } }` and rows 1 (`live`) and 2 (`gone`), `updateMany({ where: {} })` answers `count: 2`, and `update`/`remove`/`delete` of id 2 and `removeMany`/`deleteMany` all move the hidden row. `test/verbs-rules.test.ts` now snapshots through raw SQL (it was blind, FJS-2011) and pins the six cells as `D685:OFF`. A function filter such as `users: (ctx) => ({ tenantId: ctx.auth?.tenantId })` scopes a read and not a write.
  - **A** — Fold both scopes into `update`, `updateMany`, `remove`, `removeMany`, `delete` and `deleteMany` (move them from `WALKS` to `NOT_RESTORE`). The filter is then one predicate for every verb that reaches a row, matching "every path that reaches a row applies every rule that guards it". The six cells promote to `on`; an update/delete of a hidden row answers `null`/`count: 0` like an absent id. `asSystem()` still does not lift it, so a job that maintains rows the app filter hides must say so with a flag.
  - **B** — Keep writes unscoped and write it down: the filter is a READ view, access to writes is the policy and the gate (Invariant 6). The six cells become `-` with the reason; `docs/filtering.md` § Global filters says "reads" rather than "every query". The tenant function filter then needs a `@@allow` beside it to scope a write.
  - **Recommend A** — docs say "every query on a model, regardless of call site", a per-caller function filter is read by an author as scoping, and a write that ignores it is the silent hole Invariant 6 exists to refuse; B leaves the common tenant-filter case unsafe by default.

- ~~**FJS-D686 — Does "is this person connected" stay a per-process answer, or does a second API instance get a shared connection registry, and if shared, in whose store?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-09 (`FJS-D686`): A — Keep it per-process and say so in the contract: `connectionsOf` is named "on this node", the docs and `app.channels` type state it, and an app that runs more than one instance asks a service that fans the question out itself. No new mechanism; the answer stays honest but the help-desk case is not solved.** `FJS-2137`: `app.channels.connectionsOf(userId)` and `presenceOf(userId)` read this process's `connections` Map and presence Maps, so with two API instances behind a balancer a help desk's "assign only people who are connected" misses everyone whose socket landed on the other one. Nothing in junction crosses instances for sockets today: a broadcast also reaches only the sockets held by the process that announced it (`IDEAS/scaling.md` is the placement paper; `IDEAS/runtime-state-sqlite.md` proposes the runtime state as tables). Red test, if A is not taken: two `createChannelManager()` over a shared store, a socket on the first, `connectionsOf` on the second answers `[]` where it should answer the connection's id and node.
  - **A** — Keep it per-process and say so in the contract: `connectionsOf` is named "on this node", the docs and `app.channels` type state it, and an app that runs more than one instance asks a service that fans the question out itself. No new mechanism; the answer stays honest but the help-desk case is not solved.
  - **B** — A shared registry in the app's own database: a junction-owned table (`connection { id, userId, node, seenAt }`) written on open, deleted on close and swept by `seenAt` after a node dies, with `connectionsOf` answering from it. Caravan's `job_owners` heartbeat is the precedent for an instance row swept on silence (`IDEAS/scaling.md`); costs a write per connect and a staleness window after a crash, and answers only `connectionsOf` — presence and broadcast stay per node.
  - **Recommend A** — junction cannot run two instances on one port today (`Bun.serve` gets no `reusePort`, `IDEAS/scaling.md`) and a broadcast reaches only this node's sockets either way, so a shared registry would make one of three socket answers cross-node while the other two stay local. Name the answer per node now and rule B together with cross-node broadcast when multi-instance is taken up. `runtime-state-sqlite.md` does not carry that question: it proposes one `:memory:` database PER PROCESS, which is still a per-node answer.

- ~~**FJS-D688 — Is a raw `app.ws` route the door with junction's protocol taken OFF it, or an opt-in beside the browser-socket default, and which of `limits`, `upgrade`, `apiPrefix` and a per-socket slot does it carry?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-10 (`FJS-D688`): A — `app.ws` IS the raw door; no `protocol` key. `connected` and the `{type:'error'}` frames belong to the handler set carrying `SESSION_SOCKET` (the marker `FJS-1830` already reads). Third argument `{ limits?, upgrade? }`: `limits` overrides `config.http.ws` field by field for this route (a refused frame on a raw route closes 1008 with the code as reason rather than sending a frame the peer cannot read), `upgrade(req)` returns a `Response` to refuse or nothing to proceed, runs before `server.upgrade`. `app.ws` applies `apiPrefix` as `app.post` does; per-socket state is `ctx.locals`, the name service contexts already use; a handler throw goes to `onError`. Every existing `app.ws` test that pins `connected` moves in the same change.** `FJS-1829`, reproduced reading the tree 2026-10-09. `_wsOpen` (`packages/junction/src/transport/http.ts`) ends every route's open with `wsSend(ws, {type:'connected'})`; `_wsMessage` answers a rate or in-flight refusal with `{type:'error'}` and drops the frame; the limits are `this._wsLimits`, app-wide; `_handleUpgrade` refuses only on the connection caps; `ws()` pushes the path as given where `app.post` goes through `prefixPath`; `WsData` has no app slot; `try { await handlers.message } catch {}` logs nothing. The only first-party session socket registers through `app.http.ws` (`channels.ts:1276`) with `[SESSION_SOCKET]: true`, so `app.ws` has no caller that is the browser protocol. Red test: an `app.ws('/carrier', …)` route whose client receives no frame before its own first send (today it receives `{"type":"connected"}`), and whose throwing `message` handler reaches `onError`. Touches `FJS-D624` (ordering and the in-flight bound, same `_wsMessage`); the answer here should be read with it.
  - **A** — `app.ws` IS the raw door; no `protocol` key. `connected` and the `{type:'error'}` frames belong to the handler set carrying `SESSION_SOCKET` (the marker `FJS-1830` already reads). Third argument `{ limits?, upgrade? }`: `limits` overrides `config.http.ws` field by field for this route (a refused frame on a raw route closes 1008 with the code as reason rather than sending a frame the peer cannot read), `upgrade(req)` returns a `Response` to refuse or nothing to proceed, runs before `server.upgrade`. `app.ws` applies `apiPrefix` as `app.post` does; per-socket state is `ctx.locals`, the name service contexts already use; a handler throw goes to `onError`. Every existing `app.ws` test that pins `connected` moves in the same change.
  - **B** — the row's spelling: `app.ws(path, handlers, { protocol: 'raw', limits, upgrade })`, default stays the browser protocol. A route that says nothing keeps `connected`. One more key, and a default that is the wrong one for the only caller `app.ws` has.
  - **C** — a second verb for the raw door (`app.socket`), `app.ws` unchanged. Two nouns for one transport route, and `app.ws` stays a name nothing first-party uses.
  - **Recommend A** — `app.ws` has no first-party caller of the browser protocol, so the protocol is not a mode of the route but a property of the handler set that already carries a marker; an opt-in would be a flag guarding a default no one wants (*paved road vs. the workaround*), and pre-alpha has no one to keep the old `connected` for.

- ~~**FJS-D694 — How does a principal resolver that applies PRECEDENCE between two claim sources stay described, when `FJS-D522`'s array refuses a claim name emitted by two?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-09 (`FJS-D694`): A — the precedence stays a plain function, and a plain function may carry its own `describe()` returning a LIST of the claim descriptions it composes; the snapshot prints each. The `FJS-D522` array is built as ruled for the merge case, and `describe()` gains a kind for a claim proved by a signature rather than a row (`FJS-D522` already names that gap).** `FJS-1450`: Notion (Phase 3 Q3) and chatwoot (Q8) both need *no session → the link; a session on the named roster → the membership plus the link's page*. The membership claim and the `bearerClaim` both emit `workspaceId` under `strategy row` (`FJS-1731`), so the `FJS-D522` array refuses the pair and the app writes a plain function, which `junction principal` prints as *describes nothing* with *Claims: None described*. The red test: a resolver composed of a membership claim and a `bearerClaim` prints both claims in `junction principal --stdout`.
  - **A** — the precedence stays a plain function, and a plain function may carry its own `describe()` returning a LIST of the claim descriptions it composes; the snapshot prints each. The `FJS-D522` array is built as ruled for the merge case, and `describe()` gains a kind for a claim proved by a signature rather than a row (`FJS-D522` already names that gap).
  - **B** — an array element may be marked as a fallback or an override, so the array states *B only when A yields nothing*. It cannot state *a session on THIS roster*, which is the measured rule.
  - **C** — loosen `FJS-D522`: a claim name emitted by two resolvers goes to the later one. Simplest, and a claim collision made by a typo then wins in silence.
  - **Recommend A** — precedence is conditional app logic a marker cannot express, and what the row costs is the review surface, which a described list repairs with no new noun; the merge case keeps `FJS-D522` unchanged.

- ~~**FJS-D695 — Where does `record()` say WHY it has no row — `ready` rejecting, or an `error` on the view beside `get()`?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-09 (`FJS-D695`): B — the view carries `error: { status, code }` beside `get()`, and `ready` keeps resolving. The reason survives the push path as well as the first load.** `FJS-1063`: `fetch()` inside `record()` catches and resolves `null` with the reason discarded (`packages/junction/src/client/index.ts`), so a 403 and a 404 are one value and a detail screen can only say *it may not exist, or you may not have access to it*. The red test: `record(id)` over a row the caller cannot read and over an id with no row yields two distinguishable answers.
  - **A** — `ready` rejects with the error and callers catch, as `find` and `get` do. It covers the first load only: a row that becomes unreadable on a later push still has nowhere to say so.
  - **B** — the view carries `error: { status, code }` beside `get()`, and `ready` keeps resolving. The reason survives the push path as well as the first load.
  - **Recommend B** — a live view outlives its first load, so the reason belongs on the view, not the promise. (The row's other argument for B, that it moves no documented contract, carries no weight pre-alpha.)

- ~~**FJS-D696 — Does a dead bearer answer one 401 for forged, expired and revoked, or does an expired link say so?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-09 (`FJS-D696`): A — keep one message for all three. A page says *this link is no longer valid*, which names what the holder does next, ask for a new link.** `FJS-1999` shipped one `Unauthorized` message for all three from `bearerClaim` (`packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts`); its title had asked for a page that can say *this link has expired*.
  - **A** — keep one message for all three. A page says *this link is no longer valid*, which names what the holder does next, ask for a new link.
  - **B** — an expired link carries its own `code: 'expired'`; forged and revoked stay one message. It tells the holder that the token was once real.
  - **Recommend A** — the three answers lead to the same next step, and one message tells an attacker nothing about which tokens existed.

- ~~**FJS-D697 — Is an open socket by person its own read, `app.channels.connectionsOf(userId)`, beside `presenceOf`?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-09 (`FJS-D697`): A — keep `connectionsOf` beside `presenceOf`: two facts, two names.** `FJS-2001` added `connectionsOf` (the manager's live connections whose session `userId` matches, independent of channel membership) where `presenceOf` is what a client announced on a channel. Per-process only; a second API instance is filed separately.
  - **A** — keep `connectionsOf` beside `presenceOf`: two facts, two names.
  - **B** — fold it into `presenceOf(userId)` with no channel answering the open connections. One name for two facts.
  - **Recommend A** — a connection and a channel's presence differ (a help desk assigns by the first, a room shows the second), and the name says which one this is.

- **FJS-D873 — Where does a service-level (per-call) audit trail write, and what owns the declaration?** `FJS-369`: basecamp's 220-line `core/hooks.ts` trail (pre-image `before: { all }` hook, writer `after: { all }` hook, `recordable()` restating `callService`'s dispatch predicate) is what every app that wants *what did this CALL do* re-writes. The plugin is small — the ask is where its rows land. Three homes exist in the tree, and the plugin's option surface follows from the choice.
  - **A** — litestone's own trail: the plugin calls `db.$audit({ operation: '<service>.<method>', model, records, actorId, meta: { diff } })` into the schema's `driver trail` database. No new model, no new option; redaction, `[personal]`, actor kinds and the retention sweep are already litestone's (Invariant 7). Costs: `$audit` carries one `operation` string and a `meta` blob, so basecamp's typed `AuditEvent` columns (`onBehalfOfId`, `workspaceId`, the admin feed's filters) are not first-class and its feed would read the trail table instead.
  - **B** — the app declares a model and hands it to the plugin: `auditPlugin({ model: 'AuditEvent' })`, junction validating the shape at boot. Keeps basecamp's typed columns and per-workspace feed; the plugin owns the capture (`recordable()` from `callService`'s own predicate, the registry-read model, `$protectedFields` redaction, system-client re-read of both sides) and the app owns the table. Costs: a column contract junction must name and refuse on, and a second audit table beside `@@trail`.
  - **C** — the plugin ships a schema fragment, as `@frontierjs/auth` does (`schema.ts`): a `CallAudit` model the app imports. Zero declaration for the app; the table is not the app's to shape, so a workspace column or a feed filter needs an escape hatch — an option, then a second option.
  - **Recommend A** — Invariant 4 says one owner per translation and `@@trail` already owns *record that something happened, redacted*; B and C each add a second audit table to answer a column question A answers with `meta` and a query. If basecamp's feed needs typed columns, that is a litestone ask (`$audit` taking declared extra columns), which every `$audit` caller then gets.

- ~~**FJS-D812 — Where does a commitment hook run relative to a `@seals` move — before it, beside it as data, or refused at boot?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-15 (`FJS-D812`): A — reorder the one hook: it runs BEFORE the move in the same transaction, handed the row at its from-state. The move's from-state WHERE is still the lock, so a racing fire rolls its hook back with its refused move. Amends `FJS-D368`'s order; `renewPeriod` (example) reads nothing the move wrote and is unaffected.** `fireCommitment` (`junction/src/plugins/commitments/index.ts`) runs the hook after the move inside its transaction (`FJS-D368`), so on `finalize: draft -> finalized @seals @system` the hook holds a sealed row. Probed 2026-10-09 on a bare client: a hook writing an `@immutable` column throws `SealedDocumentError`, and a hook that looks up the period's draft finds none, creates a second, and the fire answers `'fired'` with two September invoices. Boot, `fli check` and `fli advise` say nothing.
  - **A** — reorder the one hook: it runs BEFORE the move in the same transaction, handed the row at its from-state. The move's from-state WHERE is still the lock, so a racing fire rolls its hook back with its refused move. Amends `FJS-D368`'s order; `renewPeriod` (example) reads nothing the move wrote and is unaffected.
  - **B** — two keyed tiers, `before` and `after`, so a hook that needs the moved row keeps it. A second spelling of one hook, for a case no current hook has.
  - **C** — the move takes the hook's data: the hook answers `data` folded into the transition's own UPDATE, so the number and the refresh land in the sealing write. Needs `transition()` to accept data, which is litestone surface.
  - **D** — refuse at boot a hook keyed to a `@seals` commitment, and the app spells a never-committed `closing` state as lago does. A refusal for a shape the framework could make work.
  - **Recommend A** — one hook, one order, and a sealing move works by construction: the owed writes land while the row is still writable, and the re-read finds the draft it is about to seal.

- ~~**FJS-D847 — Does a `@@sync` model whose policy walks a model the device does not sync get that model's schema emitted, or is it refused at build?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D847`): B — refuse at build a `@@sync` model whose policy names a model that is not `@@sync`, naming the model to add it to.** `FJS-2248`: the device schema keeps Bookmark's policies and drops Collection, so `collection.ownerId` fails to parse on the device, the device database never opens, and every offline read falls back to the list cache — while the build, `fli check` and `advise` stay green. Measured in the portal stressor 2026-09-29.
  - **A** — walk the policies when emitting the device schema and add each model they reach.
  - **B** — refuse at build a `@@sync` model whose policy names a model that is not `@@sync`, naming the model to add it to.
  - **Recommend B** — A puts a model's schema on the device without its rows, so the policy reads an empty table and answers wrong in silence; B fails loudly and is the refusal half every gap ships with.

- **FJS-D849 — How does a `@@softDelete` model in a retention database get an index the sweep can use: a spelling for an unfiltered `@@index`, or an index retention derives?** `FJS-2291`: `createIndexes` (`ddl.js`) ANDs `deletedAt IS NULL` into every `@@index` on a `@@softDelete` model, and `FJS-D719` has the sweep run unfiltered (`createdAt < cutoff` over soft-deleted rows too), so no index the model can declare is reachable by the sweep; `retention-without-an-index` fires and names no remedy. Red test: a `@@softDelete` model with `createdAt` in a `retention` database, `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN DELETE … WHERE createdAt < ?` reports `SCAN`, and no declarable `@@index` changes it.
  - **A** — retention derives it: a model with `createdAt` in a database with `retention` earns a FULL (unpartial) `@@index([createdAt])` with `generated: 'retention'`, as a relator earns its reverse index (`parser.js`, `generated: 'relator'`); `ddl.js` skips the soft-delete clause for it, and the advise rule stops asking. No new spelling, and the author never writes the index. Costs a b-tree the author did not ask for, on every model in the window.
  - **B** — a spelling for the unfiltered index: an `@@index` option (e.g. `all: true`) that suppresses the soft-delete AND. A new option for one reader, and the author must still remember to write it, which is the failure the advise rule exists for.
  - **C** — a declared `where` replaces the soft-delete clause when it names `deletedAt` (the author said it, as `@@unique(where:)` already works), and `@@index([createdAt], where: deletedAt == null or deletedAt != null)` is the spelling. No option, but a tautology as the spelling of "full", and the predicate must survive the partial-index reachability rules.
  - **Recommend A** — retention is already declared on the database and the sweep is the framework's own reader, so the framework owns its index; B and C leave the author to spell what the schema already implies.

- **FJS-D851 — How does an app say "one process, meant" so the announce-reach boot warning stops firing on every single-process app?** `FJS-2158`: `reportAnnounceReach` (`channels.ts`) warns for every sqlite database whose `announce` is not `crossProcess`, but the parser defaults `announce` to `inProcess` (`parser.js:258`), so a deliberate one-process app and a forgetful replica deploy are the same schema. Today the only silence is `crossProcess`, which costs +14 µs a write and a table (`FJS-D173`).
  - **A** — the parser records whether `announce` was written, and an explicit `announce inProcess` is the "meant" declaration that silences the report. No new keyword (the spelling already parses); the default stays quiet-on-nothing only when written.
  - **B** — soften the report to `console.info`, once, with the same text. Nothing to declare; every one-process app still prints a line at every boot, which is the trained-out-warning failure `channels.boot`'s own comment names.
  - **C** — report only when a replica signal exists (a cluster/replica env var, or a second process holding the db file). Silent default, but junction guessing at a deploy it has said it cannot see.
  - **Recommend A** — it reuses a spelling the grammar already has, makes the reach a stated fact in the schema as `FJS-D173` asks, and fires only on the app that never said; B keeps the noise and C approximates what D173 refuses to.

- ~~**FJS-D872 — Where does a notification's delivery key come from, so a retried `notify()` writes no second in-app row?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-15 (`FJS-D872`): A — the definition declares it, beside `via`: `key(payload, recipient) => \`order:${order.id}\``, the shape caravan's `dispatch({ id })` already has. Optional; a definition without one keeps today's at-least-once rows.** `notify()` delivers every transport under one `Promise.allSettled` and throws `NotificationDeliveryError` when one failed; every job in `example/` throws to make Caravan retry (`payment-announce.job.ts`, `maxAttempts: 5`), and the in-app driver (`drivers/inapp.ts` L59) creates unconditionally, so one dead mail provider is up to five bell rows per staff member per order. `audit-fanout.test.ts` holds the assertion as `test.failing`: two `app.notify(bob, new InvoiceDue())` calls, email failing both times, must leave one row for `(u-bob, contextId 42)`. Any fix adds a nullable key column to the app-copied `Notification` model (`db/notification.lite`, graded by `package-model-drift`) with a unique index on `(userId, type, key)` and has the in-app driver skip on conflict; what is open is where the key is stated. The same key decides FJS-2343 (a retry re-sends an email that already left) only if it is recorded per transport rather than on the in-app row.
  - **A** — the definition declares it, beside `via`: `key(payload, recipient) => \`order:${order.id}\``, the shape caravan's `dispatch({ id })` already has. Optional; a definition without one keeps today's at-least-once rows.
  - **B** — a call option: `app.notify(recipient, n, { key })`. The caller (the job) knows what makes the send unique; a third argument on a two-argument public verb.
  - **C** — derived, no key: the in-app driver skips when a row with the same `(userId, type, contextType, contextId)` exists. Matches the red test as written, and silently swallows every legitimate repeat (a weekly reminder about one invoice).
  - **D** — derived from the ambient call: inside a Caravan job, key = job id + recipient + type via `currentCall()`. No API surface, but protection exists only inside a job and the same send behaves differently outside one.
  - **Recommend A** — the notification is a file that already names itself and declares its transports, so the definition is where "what makes two sends the same" belongs; it reuses caravan's idempotency shape rather than coining one, stays optional so nothing silently drops, and the red test's assertion moves to a definition that declares `key`.


- **FJS-D874 — What does `@phone` normalize to, and where does a national number's region come from?** Found fixing FJS-1879. `PHONE_RE` (`validate.js:63`) accepts `'-------'` and `'(((((((('` and stores `'(555) 123-4567'` and `'+15551234567'` as two values, so a `@@unique` or a lookup keyed by a number is unique per spelling. The transform tier (`@frontierjs/toolbelt/transforms`) takes names only, with no arguments, and is shared by Litestone, Junction and Sierra; reading a national number needs a region, which nothing in a schema states today. Red test: `create` with `'(555) 123-4567'` then with `'+15551234567'` under `@unique` — the second must be refused as a duplicate, and `'-------'` must be refused as malformed.
  - **A** — `@phone` becomes a transform that normalizes to E.164 in the toolbelt table, no region: it reads only a `+`-prefixed number (strips spaces, dashes, dots, parentheses; requires 8–15 digits) and refuses a national number outright. One spelling stored and no new option, but `(555) 123-4567` stops being writable, which is the commonest way a form collects one.
  - **B** — A plus a per-field region, `@phone(region: "US")`, the named-argument shape `@time(seconds: true)` already has. A national number is read against that field's region; a `+` number ignores it. A region-less `@phone` behaves as A. Needs the parser arg, the toolbelt transform to take an argument (the table is names-only today), and `x-transforms` to carry it to the other realms.
  - **C** — B plus an app-wide default region in the schema or client config, so a form of fifty phone fields states it once. A second place a number's meaning can come from, and a precedence rule between the two.
  - **D** — normalize in Litestone only (validator tier, not the shared transforms). Smallest, but Junction and Sierra then grade the as-typed spelling while Litestone stores another, the disagreement `FJS-401` closed for the other four transforms.
  - **Opt-out (any of B–D)** — as-typed storage stays as `@phone(raw: true)`, or is not offered; the row leaves it open.
  - **Recommend B** — the region is a fact about the field, it reuses `@time(seconds: …)`'s argument shape rather than coining a mechanism, and an app-wide default (C) can be added later as a fallback without changing what a field means; skip the opt-out until an app asks, since a field that wants as-typed text is a `String`.

- **FJS-D875 — Does a `File` ref keep the public origin it was uploaded against, or does a read derive it from the storage config's current `publicBase` plus the key?** Found fixing FJS-1271. `serialize` (`storage/file-storage.js:337`) copies `config.publicBase` into every ref, and both `resolve` (`file-storage.js:351`, `ref.publicBase || ref.endpoint`) and `fileUrl` (`storage/index.js:46`) let that stored copy win, so an app that moves host or gets a CDN orphans every stored file while the `key` beside it stays valid. Red test: a client with `publicBase: 'https://a.example'` uploads; a second client over the same db with `publicBase: 'https://b.example'` reads `https://b.example/<key>`, and gets `https://a.example/<key>` today. `fileUrl(ref)` is pure and has no config, so it cannot derive anything.
  - **A** — derive at read, stop storing: `serialize` no longer writes `publicBase`; `resolve` joins the plugin's current `publicBase` to `key` for a ref on the plugin's own provider, and falls back to the ref's own `endpoint` + `bucket` for a ref whose `provider` differs (the object genuinely served elsewhere). `useStorage(config).url(ref)` passes its config; the bare `fileUrl(ref)` keeps endpoint + bucket + key and returns null without an endpoint, as it does for a local ref today. `publicBase` leaves `FileRef` and the JSON Schema (`jsonschema.js:288`, `index.d.ts:255`).
  - **B** — keep writing it, flip precedence: `resolve` prefers the config's current `publicBase` and reads the stored one only when the config has none. Smallest diff; every ref still carries a stale copy of something the config knows, and a bare `fileUrl(ref)` still answers the old host.
  - **C** — like A, but `fileUrl(ref, { publicBase })` takes the base as an explicit second argument so the pure helper can derive too. One more parameter on a public helper; callers outside the plugin must thread config through.
  - **Recommend A** — the stored copy is a second owner of a fact the config already holds (§ V.5), per-row `provider`/`endpoint` already cover the object served from somewhere else, and nothing has shipped so dropping the field costs no migration; B leaves the orphaning in `fileUrl`, C widens the helper for a caller that can use `useStorage`.

- **FJS-D876 — How does a custom method body get its `input:` type when `$` is one ambient proxy typed once?** Found fixing FJS-503. The registry half is uncontested: `typegen.js` emits `SeedTypes { TrackingUpdate: TrackingUpdate, … }` beside `ServiceTypes` (augmenting junction), and `MethodDeclaration.input` becomes `keyof SeedTypes` so a typo is a compile error, with a plain `string` fallback when no registry is augmented. The other half has no seat: `$` is `export const $: CallContext = new Proxy(...)` (`context.ts:928`), `CallContext.data` is `Record<string, unknown> | Record<string, unknown>[] | null`, and `example/api/src/services/orders.service.ts:132` reads `$.data` from a method declared `async () => …` — nothing ties that body to the `{ method, input }` entry in a different list, so indexing `SeedTypes[T]` "inside that method" cannot be done on `$`. Red test (a `tsc` fixture like `client-types.test.ts`): `const { trackingCode } = <the typed read>` must infer `string`, and `trackingCode.toFixed()` under `@ts-expect-error` must fail.
  - **A** — a typed read that names the seed type at the call: `$.input<'TrackingUpdate'>()` returns `SeedTypes['TrackingUpdate']` (the validated `ctx.data`, narrowed). The name repeats the `methods:` entry, and a wrong one compiles, so it is a hand cast with a checked spelling.
  - **B** — custom methods already receive `ctx` (`CustomMethodFn = (ctx: ServiceContext) => …`, `service.ts:1269`): declare the method as `defineMethod('TrackingUpdate', ctx => …)` so `ctx.data` is `SeedTypes[T]`, and the `methods:` entry takes that handle instead of repeating the name. Early-bound, so the body and its input cannot disagree; but it moves the `input:` name into the method (the entry's `input:` string stays for `surface.snapshot.md`), and the example's ambient-`$` style no longer applies to a typed method.
  - **C** — ship the registry and the `keyof SeedTypes` check only, and leave the body to `$.data as SeedTypes['TrackingUpdate']`. Closes the typo half, leaves the cast the row was filed against.
  - **Recommend B** — only B makes the type and the validator the same declaration; A is a cast with a nicer spelling, and C does not close the row.

- **FJS-D877 — How does junction's `ctx.locals.db` / `$.db` read the app's registered generated client, when a static litestone import under `src/` breaks every modelless app (junction `CLAUDE.md`)?** Found fixing FJS-2482. Litestone's `Register`/`RegisteredClient` (`index.d.ts:818-821`, FJS-D495 B) now types `createClient()`, but `ServiceContextLocals.db` is `LitestoneClient` (`litestone.ts:51`, every model `LitestoneTable`, rows `unknown`), so `$.db.verse.findUnique({ include })` stays untyped. The red test: with `declare module '@frontierjs/litestone' { interface Register { client: C } }` in scope, `NonNullable<ServiceContextLocals['db']>['verse']['findUnique']` must resolve to `C`'s typed row, and today it is `Promise<unknown | null>`.
  - **A** — read it by type-only dynamic reference in `litestone.ts`: `import('@frontierjs/litestone').RegisteredClient` behind a `// @ts-ignore` and an any-guard falling back to `LitestoneClient`. No new noun and no app-side step, since the `Register` augmentation typegen already emits is the only input; the cost is a `ts-ignore` and a type that is `any` for a consumer without litestone, which the guard must catch.
  - **B** — typegen `--audience system` also emits `declare module '@frontierjs/junction' { interface AppDb extends GeneratedLitestoneClient {} }` (the `--augment junction` seam), and `ctx.locals.db` / `$.db` read `AppDb` when it is augmented and `LitestoneClient` when it is empty. Junction imports nothing and `app.db` and `ctx.locals.db` finally share one typed fact; the cost is litestone's typegen naming junction in a second block (Invariant 1 holds, since it is emitted text in the app) and a union-of-empty-interface test in junction.
  - **C** — a static `import type { RegisteredClient } from '@frontierjs/litestone'`. Simplest and exact, but junction's `.ts` source ships, so a modelless consumer without litestone installed gets TS2307 on the line it never uses; it contradicts the package's optional-peer rule.
  - **Recommend B** — one augmentation point both `app.db` and `ctx.locals.db` already read (§ V.5 one owner), no `ts-ignore`, and it degrades to today's type by construction; A works but its any-guard is a second thing to keep true, and C breaks the rule that keeps junction loadable alone.

- **FJS-D884 — How does a git push reach Basecamp and start a release: a raw route with a per-App HMAC secret, a Conduit Receiver, or an unguessable per-App token URL?** Found fixing FJS-1781. `FJS-1496` is closed, so a git-source App now builds on the machine (`POST /build`) and `deployments.create` (`deployments.service.ts:147`) releases it; what is missing is the door a forge calls. `IGit.createWebhook` is `StubGit` (`providers/index.ts:86`) and no route takes a push. The red test would POST a signed push payload for the App's branch and assert one `Deployment` with `triggeredBy: null`; a wrong signature, another branch and a non-git App assert none. It cannot be written until the secret has a home: `App` has no column for it and `source` is a `Json` that `parseAppSource` strips to known keys.
  - **A** — a raw route `POST /apps/{id}/push` beside `enrollmentRoutes` (`app.ts:772`), authenticated by the forge's own HMAC (`X-Hub-Signature-256`, which Forgejo and GitHub both send) over `ctx.$raw.rawBody`, keyed by a per-App secret held as a `Secret`. `App` gains `deployOnPush Boolean` and `pushSecretId`; the route matches `ref` against `source.branch`, then calls `deployments.create` as system. One route, one verifier, the enrollment shape. `FJS-D681` says a hand-verifying route is a route and not a Receiver, which is accurate and costs nothing.
  - **B** — declare it as a Conduit `Receiver` (`FJS-D177`'s record: path, signature check, freshness, dedupe key). Conduit's `types.ts` has no Receiver yet, so this builds that noun first and moves a second consumer onto it. The right owner if more than one inbound provider is coming (PR previews in `IDEAS/paas-parity.md` need a PR-event branch of the same door); a larger first diff.
  - **C** — an unguessable per-App token in the URL (`/apps/{id}/push/{token}`), stored hashed like `enrollTokenHash`, no signature. Works with a forge that signs nothing and needs no `Secret`, but the token travels in the URL and lands in proxy logs, and nothing binds it to the payload.
  - **Recommend A** — it reuses a verifier the forges already speak and the enrollment route's shape, keeps the secret in the `Secret` store rather than a column, and moves to B unchanged if a second inbound provider appears; `createWebhook` stays a stub (a real `ForgejoGit` is its own row) and the person pastes the URL and secret into the forge.

- **FJS-D885 — How does a String column state a human sort order: a `@collate(nocase)` field word written into the DDL, or human order as the default for a `@label` / valueset `order` column?** Found fixing FJS-2500. Probed 2026-10-10: `findMany({ orderBy: [{ label: 'asc' }] })` over `Bing`, `BNI`, `apple` returns `[BNI, Bing, apple]` — SQLite BINARY collation, no `@collate` word exists, and `resource.options()` sends exactly that orderBy (`optionsOrder`, `packages/sierra/src/resource/resource.js:406`). The red test: a String column's `orderBy asc` returns `[apple, Bing, BNI]` once the schema states the order.
  - **A** — a field word `@collate(nocase)` (or `@collate(binary|nocase|rtrim)`, SQLite's three), emitted as `COLLATE NOCASE` on the column so ORDER BY, comparisons and an index all agree. The author states it per column; the DDL differ, `litestone introspect` (which already meets `COLLATE NOCASE` on adopted databases) and `explain` learn one word. NOCASE folds ASCII only, so `Émile` still sorts after `Zed` — a partial answer to "as a person reads them".
  - **B** — human order as the default for a `@label` / valueset `order` column: the compiler adds a case-insensitive collation to ORDER BY on those columns only, no new word. Nothing for the author to state, but the same column sorts differently by which clause names it, an index on the column stops serving the ORDER BY, and the rule lives in the query compiler rather than the schema (Invariant 6's direction runs the other way).
  - **Recommend A** — the order is a property of the column, so it belongs in the schema where the DDL and an index can serve it, and it matches what introspect already round-trips; a valueset or `@label` column that wants it states it once.

- ~~**FJS-D886 — Where does the onboarding for an Oracle-emitted tenant container created by `system` live?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-15 (`FJS-D886`): A — `emit()` returns a third artifact beside `text` and `document`: the `onRegistered` source for each container created by `system` that has a membership, which creates the container plus a membership carrying the role that administers it (`FJS-D623`'s answer). `fli new`/the wizard writes it into `api/` and passes it to `createLitestoneAuth`. It is derived from the answer, so the creator is stated once. Oracle's output stops being `.lite` alone.** `accessFor` (`packages/oracle/src/emit.js:359`) gives a container that has `system` and `members` a create gate of 8, and the catalog's Organization rule reads *Created by onboarding (`system`)*. `emit()` returns only the `.lite` text and the migration document, so the app it generates ships nothing that runs at that level. A fresh signup can neither create an organization nor join one (`FJS-2530`, measured in the Charter /new/ wizard). The seam is already ruled: `FJS-D520`'s `onRegistered({ user, db })` runs in the register transaction. A first membership is a blind reference, which `asSystem()` writes with no schema word. The red test: emit an answer holding a `system` container with `members`, and assert the result carries something that creates the container and its first membership on signup. Today the result holds only `text`/`document`.
  - **A** — `emit()` returns a third artifact beside `text` and `document`: the `onRegistered` source for each container created by `system` that has a membership, which creates the container plus a membership carrying the role that administers it (`FJS-D623`'s answer). `fli new`/the wizard writes it into `api/` and passes it to `createLitestoneAuth`. It is derived from the answer, so the creator is stated once. Oracle's output stops being `.lite` alone.
  - **B** — The scaffold owns it: `fli new` (and the studio wizard) installs a signup-creates-org `onRegistered` whenever the schema it receives has a `system` container with `members`, and Oracle adds a `findings` line naming the step. Oracle stays schema-only. The app layer reads the container's shape back from the schema, which is a second reader of the same fact.
  - **C** — No onboarding is generated. `emit` refuses or warns on a `system` container with no other creator and points to a written recipe. That keeps both packages small, but every generated multi-person app is still unenterable until a hand edit.
  - **Recommend A** — the answer is the one origin for who creates the container, so the hook derived from it cannot drift from the gate it has to satisfy. `FJS-D520` already fixed the hook's shape, which leaves emit a template and no design work.

- **FJS-D887 — Does a lone `Int @id` emit `AUTOINCREMENT` always, only under `@default(autoincrement())`, or never?** Found fixing FJS-2432. Probe (in-memory `createClient`): create, hard-delete, create again gives id `1` then `1` for both `id Int @id` and `id Int @id @default(autoincrement())` -- `ddl.js` `columnDef` (L312) emits `PRIMARY KEY` and the word `autoincrement()` changes nothing in the DDL. `docs/gotchas.md` § *There is no `sqlite_sequence`, and an id is reused* states the reuse as the shape, so this overrides a page, not a ruling. `@sequence` needs a `scope:` argument (`@sequence()` is a parse error), so it is no stand-in for a global counter on an `@id`.
  - **A** — every lone `Int @id` (and `BigInt @id`) emits `INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT`. One rule, no new word, a permalink never changes meaning, and a Prisma port keeps what it had. Cost: `sqlite_sequence` now exists (gotchas page flips, introspect must skip it), a small per-insert write, and an existing table needs the rebuild path to gain the keyword (pre-alpha, so a delete-and-recreate).
  - **B** — only `@default(autoincrement())` emits `AUTOINCREMENT`; a bare `Int @id` stays a plain rowid alias. The word finally means what Prisma and SQL authors read it as, and the cheap rowid stays available for a table that never exposes its id. Cost: two spellings for one-looking column, and the safe one is the one an author must remember to write (the permalink hazard remains for a bare `Int @id`).
  - **C** — never; keep reuse and point at `String @id @default(uuid())` or `@sequence(scope:)` for a stable id, leaving `gotchas.md` as the answer. Zero DDL change, and the Prisma port still loses its guarantee silently.
  - **Recommend A** — the unsafe default is the one nobody reports (§ V.9), and `Int @id` with `autoincrement()` already reads as "never reuse" to every author who types it; B keeps that same silent trap for the bare form, and the overhead SQLite documents is small against a permalink resolving to another row.

- **FJS-D893 — How does a device open a `@@sync` schema carrying `@encrypted` fields?** Found fixing FJS-2539. `createClient` with no key throws `no encryption key` (`client.js:9836`, pinned by the `audit-browser-host` control test), and with a key `#host`'s `createCipheriv` refuses by name (FJS-2398), so `host/browser.js`'s header — a local database holding the ciphertext and refusing to read it is the correct outcome — is reachable by neither path. `deviceSchema` (`src/device-schema.js`) mentions neither `@encrypted` nor `@hashed`. Red test: a `@@sync` model with an `@encrypted` field, `createBrowserClient` with no key, expected to open and refuse reads of that column; today it throws at open.
  - **A** — a device opens with ciphertext-only columns: `createClient` skips the key demand under the browser host, the column stores and returns the opaque ciphertext, and any read/write of the plaintext throws a named refusal. Makes the header true; the cost is a second client mode where an `@encrypted` column means something different.
  - **B** — `deviceSchema` drops `@encrypted`/`@hashed` fields from a `@@sync` model and returns a `lost` note per field, as it already does for other cuts. The device never holds the ciphertext, the `createClient` path is unchanged, and the header is rewritten to say so.
  - **C** — keep the refusal: a device cannot open such a schema, `deviceSchema` refuses a `@@sync` model with a protected field by name, and the header is corrected to say the outcome is a refusal at schema selection.
  - **Recommend B** — `deviceSchema` is already the one place that decides what crosses to a device and grades each cut, so the missing column is a `lost` note rather than a new client mode; A keeps ciphertext on a device that was never meant to hold it.

- **FJS-D896 — Does a `$limit` above a service's `paginate.max` get a 400 naming the max, a clamp that says it truncated, or the clamp documented as it is?** Found fixing FJS-1972. `clampPage` (`directives.ts:89`) does `Math.min(limit, maxLimit)` with no trace: `$limit=1000` against `max: 100` answers 100 rows, `total` 288 and `hasMore` true, which a caller that never reads `hasMore` takes for the whole set (ela read 100 of 260 jobs; sstime's picker offered 100 of 288 employees). The red test: `find` with `$limit=1000` on a `paginate: { max: 100 }` service over 150 rows answers 100 rows and 200 today. `clampPage` serves four callers, two of them internal (`paginate()` hook, grouped aggregate) with no bridge to refuse through.
  - **A** — the bridge refuses a `$limit` above the service's max with a 400 naming the max, as it already refuses a negative or unnamed `$` key (`FJS-D237`). A caller learns the cap on the first request and cannot read a correct-looking truncation.
