Cost: `max` becomes a contract, so the bridge must know the service's max (today only `parseQuery` and the hook do), and a client that sends a generous `$limit` to mean "as many as allowed" breaks; `clampPage` stays a clamp for internal callers.
  - **B** — keep the clamp and make it speak: the envelope gains the applied limit (`limit: 100`) beside `total`/`hasMore`, and the client's `resource.more()` already follows `hasMore`. Nothing breaks and the signal is in the response; but a caller still has to read it, which is the failure the row names.
  - **C** — keep the clamp as is and state the cap and the `hasMore` signal in junction's and sierra's `AGENTS.md`. Cheapest, and the silent truncation stays.
  - **Recommend A** — a truncation that reads as a complete answer is the failure nobody reports (§ V.9), and `FJS-D237` already refuses what the table does not name rather than absorbing it; B is the fallback if a legitimate "give me up to max" caller turns up.

- **FJS-D897 — Does a `@@transitions` move's follow-up run on every path that makes the move, or stay the commitment fire's only, with a guard on the paths that forget it?** Found fixing FJS-1995. `FJS-D368` ruled *only the fire runs a hook* (`commitments({ hooks })`, run in the fire's `$transaction` by `fireCommitment`) and rejected a litestone `onAfterWrite` reacting on every path (its B, "whether it runs inside the transaction is unmeasured"). The measured cost is chatwoot's `s.conversation.transition(id, 'reopen')` leaving the SLA clock paused, and lago's hand finalize going around `issueInvoice`. No red test exists: the code does what D368 says, so the assertion that would fail is the ruling's own clause.
  - **A** — keep D368; close the row as by design. Add to `api-hazards` that a `transition()` outside the fire owes its own follow-up, and that the app funnels every path through one function the hook also calls (lago's `issueInvoice`). Costs nothing, and the forgetting path stays silent.
  - **B** — a hook keyed by `Model.transition`, not by commitment, on the same `hooks:` option, run inside the move's transaction by every path that makes it (service, receiver, system client, fire). Needs the litestone seam to run a callback in the transition's own transaction, which `onAfterWrite` is not shown to do (measure first); the fire stops being special and `@@commitment` need not exist for a move to owe something.
  - **C** — keep the fire as the only runner and make the other paths loud: `fli check` flags a `.transition(…, '<name>')` call in an app where `commitments({ hooks })` names that model's transition, so the forgetting is caught at the diff rather than at the breach that never fires. Smaller than B; a dynamic transition name is unseen.
  - **Recommend A** — D368's reasoning (the plugin making the move is what keeps the from-state lock) still holds and B reopens a rejected option on two measured hand-path misses that one shared function already fixes in lago; revisit B only when a third stressor needs a hook on a move no commitment ever fires.

- **FJS-D898 — How does an update learn a `@capability` column's stored value, so restating it (null on an already-null row) is not charged the column grant?** Found fixing FJS-2570. `CapabilityPlugin._checkUpdate` runs in `onBeforeUpdate`, before the stored row is read, so presence of the key is the only test; the create half was fixed by comparing to what omission stores (`storesWhatOmissionStores`, `client.js`). Red test: `as(db, 'Offer.update').offer.update({ where: { id }, data: { signedName: null } })` on a row whose `signedName` is already null rejects `/Offer\.signedName/` today.
  - **A** — the plugin reads the stored row itself in `_checkUpdate` through `ctx.tables` and skips a key whose named value equals it. One file, but the read and the UPDATE are two statements outside the unit, so a row changed in between lets a caller overwrite a value they were never granted.
  - **B** — the grant is checked where the stored row is already in hand: `updatePlan` reads the row synchronously with the statement and no await between, so the check is atomic. Needs a plugin seam (a second hook, or `onBeforeUpdate` handed a `stored()` the plan calls), and a read the common path does not pay today.
  - **C** — keep presence as the test, close the row as by design, and say in `data-hazards` that a hand-written update restates only what changed. Sierra's patch already sends only changed keys, so a generated form is unaffected.
  - **Recommend C** — the only caller hurt is a hand-written update restating a value it was not granted, which can name fewer keys; A opens a check-then-write race on an access rule and B adds a plugin seam and a read to every capability update for it.

- ~~**FJS-D899 — What fields does a conduit Receiver hold, and which are the connector's, the app's and conduit's?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-15 (`FJS-D899`): C — the connector's Dialect, the app's Receiver, conduit's order and applied-not-seen receipts, kept in conduit's store.** Found fixing FJS-1804. `FJS-D681` ruled the Receiver's record as *path, signature check, freshness window, dedupe key*, and conduit has no Receiver yet (`rg -i receiver packages/conduit/src` finds one comment). Three apps hand-built it (quo `providers/receiver.ts`, serpgrid `core/receiver.ts`, calendly `calendar-hook.ts`) and each needed steps § B lacks: `credential(delivery)` chosen BEFORE verify (Quo's key is the subscription's row), `parse(delivery, cred)`, `dedupeKey(delivery, event, cred)` (a header in Standard Webhooks), `owner(event, cred)` (whose tenant, found after verify), and `apply` answering applied/parked/ignored with dedupe meaning APPLIED (`FJS-1805`). Graph adds a handshake, batched per-item deliveries and lifecycle events (`FJS-2573`). The red assertion: a Quo Standard Webhooks delivery cannot be declared in `path · verify · window · dedupe`, because the key is chosen by the path's row and the dedupe key is a header.
  - **A** — one record carrying all seven steps (`path · credential · verify · window · parse · dedupeKey · owner · apply`), conduit owning the pipeline order and the route, so quo's and serpgrid's `receive()` is deleted and not ported. One declaration per Provider, but a connector now writes the app's tenancy (`owner`) and domain (`apply`) into a vendor package, and Graph's handshake and batch widen the record again.
  - **B** — keep § B's four (`path · verify · window · dedupe`) and hand the app a verified delivery; `credential`, `parse`, `owner` and `apply` stay app code. Smallest, and `FJS-D681`'s text stands, but the three dialects above do not fit the dedupe field, and each app rewrites the record-then-apply-then-undo order `FJS-1805` found wrong once.
  - **C** — split by owner: the connector declares the dialect (`verify · window · parse · dedupeKey`, pure functions of a delivery and a credential), the app declares the receiver (`path · credential · owner · apply`) binding a dialect, and conduit owns the route, the pipeline order and the applied-not-seen dedupe. Handshake, batching and lifecycle events stay out until a second app asks, since `FJS-2573` owns the subscription's life. Dialect code stays in the connector (`FJS-D153`) and tenancy stays in the app.
  - **Recommend C** — each field lands with the party that knows it (a vendor knows its signature, only the app knows its tenants), and conduit owns the one thing three apps got wrong or copied: the order and what dedupe means; A puts tenancy into connectors and B leaves that order to every app.

- **FJS-D900 — How does a client opened on a database that is ahead of its schema find out?** `FJS-754`: `createClient` on a tenant file another process migrated forward says nothing, and the column is invisible to it. The red test is `createClient({ schema: behind, db })` after `autoMigrate` of the ahead schema, with `console.warn` captured: it asserts one announcement naming the extra column, and today there is none (`test/schema-behind.test.ts` `behindBy` is the setup). The hash cannot decide it (`readAutoHash` carries no ordering and differs on every dev edit); `onlyDrops` can, but only after the pristine `:memory:` build the hash exists to skip.
  - **A** — superset test at open: `PRAGMA table_info` per table against the schema's columns; any live column the schema lacks (and nothing the schema adds that the live lacks) announces once per client. No stored-format change. Costs one PRAGMA per table on every `createClient`, which a tenant-per-file app pays per tenant open, and it also fires in dev for a column just removed from the schema and not yet migrated.
  - **B** — record an ordered stamp (a schema generation counter or the migration count) beside the hash in the auto-meta table, and compare at open. Exact ordering, one row read, no false positive on a dev edit. A stored-format change, and an old database carries no stamp until its next migration.
  - **C** — do nothing at open; keep `autoMigrate`'s refusal (`FJS-566`) as the only voice, and document that a process which never calls it never learns. Zero cost, and the gap stays as the row describes it.
  - **Recommend A, run only on the first open of each file and silent when `autoMigrate` follows in the same boot** — it needs no format change and the cost is one PRAGMA per table; measure it on a 100-table schema before building, and fall back to B if a tenant open cannot afford it.

- **FJS-D901 — What does an HTTP concurrency cap count, what is it called, and does it belong to junction or the edge?** `FJS-707`'s one remaining item: sockets have `ws.maxConnections` (checked in the upgrade at `http.ts` ~1277 against `stats.performance.online`) and HTTP has nothing. Bun gives HTTP no connection hook — `Bun.serve` offers no accept/close callback, so an idle keep-alive socket cannot be counted — only the requests in hand, which `HttpTransport._inFlight` (`_handle`, `http.ts` ~598) already counts for graceful shutdown. The row's own constraint holds either way: the default is unlimited, because only an operator knows what the box holds (`FJS-705`). The red test: with the cap set to 2, three concurrent slow requests, the third answers 503 today it is served.
  - **A** — `http.maxInFlight`, a count of requests being handled, refused in `_handle` before any work as `503` + `Retry-After` + the `retryable: true` body `requestTimeout` already uses, off by default, counted into `stats.response.error`. Honest name for what Bun lets us count; a WS upgrade stays under `ws.maxConnections`, and the two are never summed.
  - **B** — `http.maxConnections`, same mechanism as A but named for the sibling `ws.maxConnections`. Predictable by name, and wrong: it caps requests, not connections, so an operator who sets it against a connection count is told something false.
  - **C** — junction does not cap; the edge does. `packages/cli/core/edge.js` emits no `limit_conn` today, so the shipped nginx would gain a `limit_conn` zone and `fli` the option that sets it. A real connection cap, outside the process that is already saturated, and nothing to maintain in the transport — but an app run without the edge has none, and `bun run` apps are the majority in dev.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the only cap the process can enforce and measure truthfully, it reuses a counter that exists, and C can be added later beside it without changing it.

- **FJS-D902 — Does the outbox promise delivery order, and if so per what key — or is unordered at-least-once the documented contract?** `FJS-893`: `deliverOutbox` claims owed rows `createdAt asc` but a failed row waits out its backoff while later rows are dispatched under their own ids and go past it; `ctx.enqueue(job, payload)` carries no ordering key and `OutboxMessage` has no column for one. Neither `example` nor `basecamp` enqueues anything order-sensitive today, so no caller is hurt yet.
  - **A** — unordered is the contract: say it in the `deliverOutbox` comment and in `api-hazards` (*a handler must not assume it sees an aggregate's effects in write order; carry a version or a timestamp in the payload and reconcile*), and close the row as by design. No new noun, no head-of-line blocking, a dead row still stalls only itself.
  - **B** — an optional `ctx.enqueue(job, payload, { orderKey })`: the row stores the key, and the relay delivers a key's rows one at a time, in `createdAt` order, each only once its predecessor is delivered. Ordered where asked and free elsewhere, but it adds an option and a column, a key's dead row stalls the whole key until an operator clears it, and the row must say what `maxAttempts` exhaustion does to the rows behind it.
  - **C** — ordering is the app's own job through a conduit target of kind broker (`FJS-D235`), where a partition key is the broker's native concept; the outbox stays unordered and the broker is where ordered consumers live.
  - **Recommend A** — no app here needs order, a key nobody has declared is a feature built for a guess, and B can be added later beside the unordered path without changing it; revisit when a stressor app names an aggregate whose effects must land in sequence.

- **FJS-D906 — Does FJS-D466's bound hold for an unordered live list whose window already holds the query's end?** FJS-2617 made an ORDERED window that holds the query's last row grow by a created row rather than trim one, since there is no page 2 for the overflow to move to. The unordered branch of `insert` (`packages/junction/src/client/index.ts`) still answers FJS-D466: at its page size it counts the row on `stale` and bumps `total`, so a drained unordered list reports `hasMore()` true while `more()` has no cursor and returns `[]` at once — a `while (list.hasMore) await list.more()` loop spins. `live-order.test.ts` › "an unordered list at its page size COUNTS a new row rather than growing" pins the bound with `total: 2, limit: 2`, which is exactly a window holding the end.
  - **A** — Grow there too: when `total`, or an explicit `hasMore: false`, says the window holds the end, the row is in the query and in the window, so append it. FJS-D466's 3003-row measurement then applies only where the server never stated an end.
  - **B** — Keep the bound, but do not count the row on `total` when the window holds the end: `stale` says *refresh*, and `hasMore()` stays false because `more()` cannot reach the row.
  - **Recommend A** — a window that holds the whole query is the query (FJS-D145), so a row that enters the query enters the window, and growth is bounded by the query, not the push count.

- ~~**FJS-D907 — What is the re-presentment cadence for a soft-declined subscription invoice, and what holds the attempt count and the clock?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D907`): A — a fixed short ladder in the app, `RETRY_DAYS = [1, 3, 5, 7]` after each soft decline, attempt number counted from the invoice's failed `Payment` rows (no new column), each dispatch `{ delay, unique: occurrenceKey('collect', invoiceId, attempt) }`. Four tries, not twenty-one; stops at the first paid, hard decline or non-`issued` invoice. Nothing stamped, so a shop changing the ladder moves unfired retries.** Found fixing FJS-610. `payments.record` on `payment.failed` with a soft `declineKind` returns `recorded` and dispatches nothing (`payments.service.ts` ~475), and `invoice-collect` is dispatched once, by `Invoice`'s renewal hook (`commitments.ts` ~31). Caravan's ladder cannot cover it: a decline is a returned answer, not a throw. The red test: record a soft `payment.failed` for an `issued` subscription invoice and assert one `invoice-collect` is queued with a `delay` and a `unique` key naming the attempt (`FJS-609`); today none is. The window already exists — `DUNNING_DAYS` 21, `GRACE_DAYS` 3, frozen on each invoice (`billing.ts` ~40-52) — so the cadence has to fit inside it.
  - **A** — a fixed short ladder in the app, `RETRY_DAYS = [1, 3, 5, 7]` after each soft decline, attempt number counted from the invoice's failed `Payment` rows (no new column), each dispatch `{ delay, unique: occurrenceKey('collect', invoiceId, attempt) }`. Four tries, not twenty-one; stops at the first paid, hard decline or non-`issued` invoice. Nothing stamped, so a shop changing the ladder moves unfired retries.
  - **B** — the ladder is stamped on the invoice at issue like `graceDays` (`retryDays`, an `Int[]`-shaped column or a count plus interval), so terms already given never move (`FJS-D355`). Truest to the invoice owning its deadline, but a new frozen column for a schedule the customer is never told.
  - **C** — a `@@commitment` on `Invoice` (`retry`, `on: dueOn + N`, `while: status == 'issued'`) whose hook dispatches the collect, so the retry clock is the same graded, durable machinery as dunning and survives a lost queue. One commitment fires once per N, so a ladder needs several, and each needs a stamped day count.
  - **Recommend A** — the attempt count already lives in the `Payment` rows and the ladder is a shop habit rather than a term (the `remind` commitment's `3d` is a literal for the same reason); a short ladder keeps clear of the network-scrutiny the row names, and B or C can replace it without changing the dispatch's `unique` shape.

- ~~**FJS-D908 — How does a per-row zone (a column, possibly two relations away) reach a `where`?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D908`): A — a zone-aware operator the client lowers: the caller states the wall-clock rule and the zone field (`{ startsAt: { localTime: { atOrBefore: '08:00', zone: 'inviteeTimeZone' } } }`); litestone selects the distinct zones present (`SELECT DISTINCT zone`, bounded and indexable), computes each zone's instant bound with `fromWall`/`partsIn`, and lowers to `(zone = ? AND startsAt <= ?) OR …`. SQL stays instant-only, so `FJS-D143` stands and the cost is one DISTINCT plus an index range per zone. The zone must be a column of the same row; a zone two relations away (`Shift → Employee → User`) needs a join the lowering does not do today, so that case stays open or is solved by A + a denormalized zone column. Adds an operator to `where`, `$checkWhere` and the directive table.** `FJS-1234`: *has 08:00 local passed for this row* names a zone that differs per row, so the sweep bounds `startsAt` by an instant window and asks the real question in JavaScript — measured 566 rows read for 96 sent in one fire, 165,744 read for 173 sent over a day, set by the horizon alone. `FJS-D143` ruled a zoned comparison is *a window the framework binds and never a SQL predicate* (`datetime('now','America/New_York')` answers NULL), but its window is ONE zone per evaluation (the viewer's, the tenant's, a sibling column of the same row); no `where` today takes a zone that varies per row. The red test: a model with `startsAt` and a zone column, rows across three zones, `where` for *wall time 08:00 on startsAt's local day has passed* returns only the due rows — nothing in `where` can state it, so the test cannot be written without choosing the spelling.
  - **A** — a zone-aware operator the client lowers: the caller states the wall-clock rule and the zone field (`{ startsAt: { localTime: { atOrBefore: '08:00', zone: 'inviteeTimeZone' } } }`); litestone selects the distinct zones present (`SELECT DISTINCT zone`, bounded and indexable), computes each zone's instant bound with `fromWall`/`partsIn`, and lowers to `(zone = ? AND startsAt <= ?) OR …`. SQL stays instant-only, so `FJS-D143` stands and the cost is one DISTINCT plus an index range per zone. The zone must be a column of the same row; a zone two relations away (`Shift → Employee → User`) needs a join the lowering does not do today, so that case stays open or is solved by A + a denormalized zone column. Adds an operator to `where`, `$checkWhere` and the directive table.
  - **B** — a materialized instant: a stored column the app or a hook keeps current (`remindAt`), plain `where remindAt <= now`, fully indexable, any relation depth because the app writes it. No litestone change at all and `@generated` stays deterministic and same-row (`parser.js` ~973); the cost is that the rule lives in app code beside the write, goes stale when the zone or the rule changes, and `tooLate` (shifts starting before 08:00 local) is still the app's to define.
  - **C** — a framework-bound window per zone, not per row: litestone exposes the *per-zone bound* as a helper (`db.booking.zonedBounds({ zone: 'inviteeTimeZone', wall: '08:00' })` returning `[{ zone, instant }]`) and the app composes the OR itself. Smallest surface and no operator, but every app re-writes the same OR and `$checkWhere` cannot see it, so it is Invariant 4's *beside the owner* by another route.
  - **Recommend A, same-row zone only** — it is the only choice that makes the database do the narrowing from a declaration, it keeps `FJS-D143`'s *SQL never evaluates a zone* intact (the zones are enumerated, then every comparison is an instant), and `FJS-1214` asks for the same word in declaration so the spelling is shared; B is the workaround an app can already use today and the row's two-relation case should be answered by it (or by a later join-lowering), not by widening A.

- ~~**FJS-D909 — Where does the one doc-fence reader live and how does a package supply the prelude its examples run under?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D909`): A — One reader in `@frontierjs/toolbelt` (a `docfences` kit: fences with `lang`, `words`, `line`, nearest preceding schema). Litestone's runner, `runnableFences` and `doc-audit` all import it; each package keeps its own `test/doc-examples.test.*` supplying its prelude, and `doc-examples-run` keeps grading the wiring. Toolbelt is below the graph (`FJS-D26`) so any package may import it; the cost is that `FJS-D860`'s "moves to `fli check`" is amended to "moves to toolbelt".** `FJS-2331`: three fence readers exist (litestone `doc-examples.test.ts` `blocksOf`, `checks.js` `runnableFences`, `doc-audit.js` `fences`), the fence contract (`fragment`, `throws`, `failing`) is package-neutral, and 19 packages fence runnable examples with no runner. `FJS-D860` says the extraction moves to `fli check`, but a package's own test cannot import `@frontierjs/cli` (Invariant 1's direction: cli sits above every package), and `fli check` cannot build a prelude (a client from the nearest schema, `createTestApp`, a compiled component) without being told how.
  - **A** — One reader in `@frontierjs/toolbelt` (a `docfences` kit: fences with `lang`, `words`, `line`, nearest preceding schema). Litestone's runner, `runnableFences` and `doc-audit` all import it; each package keeps its own `test/doc-examples.test.*` supplying its prelude, and `doc-examples-run` keeps grading the wiring. Toolbelt is below the graph (`FJS-D26`) so any package may import it; the cost is that `FJS-D860`'s "moves to `fli check`" is amended to "moves to toolbelt".
  - **B** — `fli check` owns the reader AND the run: a package declares `test/doc-prelude.{js,ts}` (default export `(block) => { imports, head }`), `fli check` writes each unmarked fence as a module under that prelude and runs it. One runner for 20 packages, no per-package test file; the cost is a new file convention plus a process fleet inside `fli check`, and the prelude for a drive-needing package (mesa served page, sierra build) is not a head string.
  - **C** — Reader in `packages/cli/core`, package runners import it by relative path from the workspace, the way `@frontierjs/ui` reads mesa's CDP harness. No new package surface, but it makes every package's suite depend on cli, a reverse edge.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the only choice with one reader and no reverse edge, it keeps `FJS-D860`'s "the package owns its words" (the prelude), and the 19 runners then each become a small test over a shared reader instead of 19 copies of litestone's 226 lines.

- ~~**FJS-D910 — Are `$or`/`$and`/`$not` wire vocabulary, or in-process only?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D910`): A — Name them in the toolbelt table as a third class beside DIRECTIVES and TRANSPORT_PARAMS (`COMBINATORS`): `unknownDirectives` skips them, `splitParams` leaves them in `ctx.query`, and Invariant 10 is amended to say a combinator is a filter written with a `$`. `?$or[][name]=a` and `{ $or: [...] }` over WS both work; the dedupe of the three lists (`match.js`, `litestone.ts`, the table) collapses into one.** `FJS-2213`: `refuseBadDirectives({ $or: [{ name: 'a' }] })` throws `$or is not a directive` (`unknownDirectives` holds only the directives table plus transport params), while `matchesQuery` (`match.js:267-272`) and `parseWhere` (`litestone.ts:377-385`) both implement them and `parseQuery` exempts them (`WHERE_COMBINATORS`, `litestone.ts:200`). Invariant 10 says no `$`-prefixed key survives the bridge and `ctx.query` is filters, so the code that reads combinators and the invariant disagree. Red test: `refuseBadDirectives({ $or: [{ name: 'a' }] })` must not throw, or `matchesQuery` must stop reading `$or`.
  - **A** — Name them in the toolbelt table as a third class beside DIRECTIVES and TRANSPORT_PARAMS (`COMBINATORS`): `unknownDirectives` skips them, `splitParams` leaves them in `ctx.query`, and Invariant 10 is amended to say a combinator is a filter written with a `$`. `?$or[][name]=a` and `{ $or: [...] }` over WS both work; the dedupe of the three lists (`match.js`, `litestone.ts`, the table) collapses into one.
  - **B** — Remove them from the wire: delete the `$or`/`$and`/`$not` branches from `matchesQuery` and keep `parseWhere`'s only for in-process callers that build `where` themselves. Invariant 10 stands as written; a client needing OR over a transport calls a custom service method.
  - **Recommend A** — OR/NOT filters are the commonest thing a list screen needs beyond equality, `matchesQuery` already grades them for live stores, and the litestone `where` grammar is the same everywhere; Invariant 10's point (a `$` key is not a column) survives because the table names exactly three combinators and still refuses every other `$` name.

- ~~**FJS-D911 — How does a parent's column reach a child's `@derived` or `@from`?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D911`): B — ship the to-one `@from(rel, column)` form: when the first argument names a to-one relation field on this model and the second a column on its target, `parseFrom` returns a `{ rel, column }` op and the client compiles it with the correlated scalar subquery a policy's `relation.column` path already builds. `@from` stays the one owner of "bring a value onto this row", the refusal's promise becomes true, and the `.some` refusal keeps its `exists: true` cure.** `FJS-1305`: `@derived(state.category)` is refused (`policy.js` `case 'path'`) with *bring the value onto this model with `@from(state, category)`*, and that spelling does not parse (`parseFrom` wants `target, op: value`; `Expected COLON, got ')'`). `@from(WorkflowState, first: true)` reads DOWN a to-many, so a parent's column cannot be brought onto a child at all. `policy-paths.test.ts` "@derived refuses it and points at @from" asserts the unparseable cure today.
  - **A** — correct the message only: say a `@derived` field does not cross a relation and name no cure (a stored copy, or a `post-update` `@@deny` for the same-team check). Smallest change, but the capability stays missing and the Linear schema keeps a copied `category` that goes stale when a state is re-categorised.
  - **B** — ship the to-one `@from(rel, column)` form: when the first argument names a to-one relation field on this model and the second a column on its target, `parseFrom` returns a `{ rel, column }` op and the client compiles it with the correlated scalar subquery a policy's `relation.column` path already builds. `@from` stays the one owner of "bring a value onto this row", the refusal's promise becomes true, and the `.some` refusal keeps its `exists: true` cure.
  - **C** — let `@derived` itself accept a one-hop path (`@derived(state.category)`), routing `case 'path'` to the same subquery. The spelling the author reaches for first, but it is the second way to cross a relation that the `policy.js` comment says this language exists to avoid, and `@derived` stops being same-row.
  - **Recommend B** — `@from` is already the declared owner of crossing a relation onto a row and the refusal already promises it; B adds the missing to-one direction on existing machinery with no second origin.

- ~~**FJS-D912 — Who derives the cross-origin list — the web domain's `https://` origin plus a wrapped shell's `tauri://localhost` / `http://tauri.localhost` — and where does it land?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D912`): A — `fli deploy` derives it and hands it to the API as one framework-level env (`FJS_CORS_ORIGINS`: `https://<web.domain>` plus the shell schemes of each wrapped surface), which `applyConfiguredMiddleware` merges into `middleware.cors.origins` and `csrf.origins`. One new reader in junction, no per-app code; the OS split lives in one cli table beside `desktop-surface.js`.** Found fixing FJS-2655. Measured first (Chrome, `app.example.com` → `api.example.com`, two ports): a HOST-ONLY `HttpOnly` cookie set by the API rides a `credentials: 'include'` fetch from the app origin under `SameSite=Lax` and `Strict` alike, same as `Domain=example.com`, so no cookie-Domain setting or refusal is needed; what is missing is the list. Today `app.ts` only warns at boot, and the one app that lists shell origins (`packages/basecamp/api/config/junction.config.js`) writes them by hand and overrides through a basecamp-only `CORS_ORIGINS`. `deploy.web.domain` and the desktop surface are `cli` facts; junction cannot read them (Invariant 1).
  - **A** — `fli deploy` derives it and hands it to the API as one framework-level env (`FJS_CORS_ORIGINS`: `https://<web.domain>` plus the shell schemes of each wrapped surface), which `applyConfiguredMiddleware` merges into `middleware.cors.origins` and `csrf.origins`. One new reader in junction, no per-app code; the OS split lives in one cli table beside `desktop-surface.js`.
  - **B** — a toolbelt helper `appOrigins({ webDomain, shells })` that an app's `junction.config.js` calls, so the list is still written in the app but never spelled by hand. No env, no junction change; each app still has to pass the domain.
  - **C** — build nothing: the boot notice stays, and the measured cookie fact is recorded as a hazard. Apps keep listing origins in `junction.config.js` as basecamp does.
  - **Recommend A** — the web domain is declared once, in the deploy config, and a second hand-written copy in `junction.config.js` is the drift this row exists for; A reuses the env mechanism basecamp already proves, lifted from one app to the framework.

- ~~**FJS-D913 — How does Studio get `@frontierjs/css` and its fonts without a CDN, given litestone depends on toolbelt alone?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D913`): A — Vendor a generated copy of `frontier.min.css` (~88 KB) at `src/tools/studio.css`, text-imported and served by Studio at its own path; a `packages/css` build step writes it and a litestone test fails when the copy differs from `packages/css/dist`. Fonts drop to the system stack (Inter and JetBrains Mono stay first in the list for machines that have them). No dependency edge, works in a compiled binary, costs one generated file.** Found fixing FJS-2665. `studio.html` links `cdn.jsdelivr.net/npm/@frontierjs/css@0.15.0/dist/frontier.min.css` and `@import`s Google Fonts; a `--host` Studio with no egress renders unstyled. Studio is one text-imported file (`studio.js:24`) so a compiled binary carries it, and `@frontierjs/css` is a UI package litestone may not depend on (Invariant 1, `FJS-D635`). Red test: the served page contains no `https://` stylesheet or font URL.
  - **A** — Vendor a generated copy of `frontier.min.css` (~88 KB) at `src/tools/studio.css`, text-imported and served by Studio at its own path; a `packages/css` build step writes it and a litestone test fails when the copy differs from `packages/css/dist`. Fonts drop to the system stack (Inter and JetBrains Mono stay first in the list for machines that have them). No dependency edge, works in a compiled binary, costs one generated file.
  - **B** — Studio resolves `@frontierjs/css` at runtime when it is installed (optional peer) and serves `dist/frontier.min.css` from it, keeping the CDN link as the fallback. No copy to drift, but a data-only app has no css installed, so the offline case this row is about still renders unstyled, and a compiled binary cannot resolve it.
  - **C** — Leave the CDN and record it: Studio already works unstyled (`studio.html` header), and `--host` on an offline machine is the rare case. Smallest change; the row closes as a documented limit.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the only option that fixes the offline case without a dependency edge; the drift is checked by a test rather than by memory, and dropping web fonts for the system stack removes the second CDN call with no asset to carry.

- ~~**FJS-D914 — When a custom method answers another model's row, how does the announcement learn which model it is — a per-method declaration, or a refusal to guess?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D914`): A — `answers: 'Task'` on the `methods:` entry (a model name, validated at boot like `model:`). `announcementPayload` and `announceToChannels` take that accessor for this method, so the completeness check, the re-read and the per-recipient grading all use Task's columns and policy. One declaration, derived everywhere else; the cost is a second place a `model` can be named on a service, beside `FJS-D628`'s `model:`.** `FJS-2012`: `announcementPayload` (`core/litestone.ts`) compares the answer's keys to the SERVICE's accessor columns, so `messages.promote()` answering a Task warns *answered 22 of 17 columns* and re-reads a MESSAGE by the task's id (`tableFor(client, accessor)` + `findFirst({ where: { [idField]: id } })`) — a wrong row, or a stranger's, on the messages channel. Grading is on the declared model too (`announceToChannels` → `serviceAccessor`, `FJS-D175`), so a Task row would be graded by Message's read policy. Red test: a service over `Message` with a `methods:` entry that returns a `Task` row; assert the `messages:promoted` payload is that Task, not a Message re-read by `task.id`. Today the only escape is `$.dispatch = false`, which drops the announcement.
  - **A** — `answers: 'Task'` on the `methods:` entry (a model name, validated at boot like `model:`). `announcementPayload` and `announceToChannels` take that accessor for this method, so the completeness check, the re-read and the per-recipient grading all use Task's columns and policy. One declaration, derived everywhere else; the cost is a second place a `model` can be named on a service, beside `FJS-D628`'s `model:`.
  - **B** — No declaration: a custom method's answer is announced as-is and never re-read when its id/shape does not match the service's model (skip the re-read for any custom method, keep it for CRUD verbs). Nothing to learn, but the broadcast is then graded against the wrong model's policy, so the announcement must also be withheld from channels unless `$.dispatch` states a payload — the same silent loss as today.
  - **C** — Refuse to guess, by name: a custom method whose answer has more keys than the service's model, or whose declared output type is another model, throws at boot/first call naming `answers:`-or-`$.dispatch`. No new option, but it makes a legitimate method a hard failure.
  - **Recommend A** — the grading rule (`FJS-D175`) already says the DECLARED model is the accessor, and a method that answers another model's row is declaring one more fact about itself; naming it keeps the check, the re-read and the grade on one accessor instead of three guesses.

- ~~**FJS-D922 — Does a declared extensible key with no slot get filtered by a scan, as `extensible-columns.md` § The thirteenth field says, or refused, as `rewriteExtensibleWhere` does — and if refused, how does a filter UI learn which keys it may offer?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D922`): A — Honor the doc: a slotless key compiles to `json_extract(<blob>, ?)` with the key bound as a path parameter (Invariant 8), ANDed with the scope the query already carries. Slower, never wrong; the "widens in silence" worry that justified refusal applies to dropping a term, not to scanning it. The refusal test flips to a scan test; an undeclared key is still refused by name.** Found fixing FJS-1757. The doc says a slotless key "stores, displays and edits like any other, and only a query naming it falls back to a scan"; `rewriteExtensibleWhere` (`core/client.js`) throws `'k' is declared but holds no slot …` and `test/extensible.test.ts` ("a declared key with no slot is refused rather than scanned") pins it. Red assertion for A: `findMany({ where: { fields: { notes: 'x' } } })` resolves to the rows whose blob holds `notes = 'x'` instead of rejecting. `$declaredFields()` answers slot but no "filterable" fact, so under refusal nothing tells a tenant's filter UI which keys to offer.
  - **A** — Honor the doc: a slotless key compiles to `json_extract(<blob>, ?)` with the key bound as a path parameter (Invariant 8), ANDed with the scope the query already carries. Slower, never wrong; the "widens in silence" worry that justified refusal applies to dropping a term, not to scanning it. The refusal test flips to a scan test; an undeclared key is still refused by name.
  - **B** — Keep the refusal and correct the doc: § The thirteenth field says "stores and displays; filtering needs a slot", and `$declaredFields()` rows gain a derived `filterable` (slot held) so a filter UI offers only those keys. Nothing slow can be asked for, but a tenant past the pool size cannot filter on a key they declared, and the Q6 measurement (most realistic declarations hold no slot) says that is most keys.
  - **C** — A: scan for a slotless key, plus `$declaredFields()` marks `indexed` per key so a UI can warn rather than hide. Most code and the widest surface.
  - **Recommend A** — it is what the doc promises and what a tenant expects; the failure refusal guards (a silently wider answer) is not a property of a scan, and the Q6 numbers say refusal would make filtering unavailable for the common case.

- ~~**FJS-D938 — Which processes does the `@@commitment` authoring check refuse, and how does a queue-less test app fire what is owed?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D938`): A — The check asks a process that KEEPS TIME: `start()` always, and any boot of an app configuring `caravan` (the queue the sweep runs on). An in-process app with neither is a harness whose test is the clock, and `fireDue(app, { hooks, timeZone })` in `@frontierjs/junction/commitments` fires every row due by the client's clock, inline, through the plugin's own fire. Derived from the start mode and the configured plugins; no flag.** `check-authoring` refused every boot of a schema declaring a commitment with no `commitments()` configured, `_startForTest()` included, so ELA's one `expire` on `Document` broke the 31 test files that build `createApp` in-process over the real schema with no queue, and `@frontierjs/junction/testing` had nothing to stand in (`FJS-2708`). `commitments()` requires caravan, and `app.commitments.sweep()` only dispatches to it.
  - **A** — The check asks a process that KEEPS TIME: `start()` always, and any boot of an app configuring `caravan` (the queue the sweep runs on). An in-process app with neither is a harness whose test is the clock, and `fireDue(app, { hooks, timeZone })` in `@frontierjs/junction/commitments` fires every row due by the client's clock, inline, through the plugin's own fire. Derived from the start mode and the configured plugins; no flag.
  - **B** — Refuse only under `start()`; `_startForTest()` and `_startOnce()` never ask. Simplest sentence, but a suite that boots the real composition — caravan and all — never sees the refusal, which is `check-attachments`' warning: the check that only runs when serving is the one nobody sees fail.
  - **C** — Keep the refusal everywhere and ship a double in `@frontierjs/junction/testing` (a `commitments` stand-in with no queue) that every test app configures. One line per test file, 31 times in ELA, for wiring that tests nothing.
  - **Recommend A** — it keeps the refusal under the test runner for every app composed like the deployed one, waives it for exactly the app that runs nothing on its own clock, and gives that app's test the fire it needs without a second fire path.

- ~~**FJS-D950 — Is a ratio between two counts a refusal, or a state the data may be in?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-13 (`FJS-D950`): B — a flag the row carries: `underRatio Boolean @derived(adults * eventRatioAttendees < eventEnrolled * eventRatioAdults)` on Shift, the event's three values brought on by `FJS-D911`'s to-one `@from`. Every read says it, `where: { underRatio: true }` lists the rooms to fix, and SRC's badge is that field. No new word, once the three rows above close; it refuses nothing, and *answering for the room on the day* (a `@@commitment` at the shift's start, or a report) is a later question. **Owes its artefact, because B is wrong in silence exactly as `FJS-2778` measured** (a `@derived` over a `@from` reads one answer and filters another): `ratio.test.ts` flips to read `underRatio` off every shift and through `where: { underRatio: true }`, and asserts both agree with SRC's `ratioInfo`.** Stressor #18 (eventmark) Q2, measured in `fjs-prototypes/eventmark/api/test/ratio.test.ts`. `@ratio 1:4` holds a shift's adults (`Shift.adults @from(Assignment, count: true)`) against its event's enrollment (`Event.enrolled @from(Enrollment, count: true)`): under ratio is `adults * ratioAttendees < enrolled * ratioAdults`. Two writes on two models break it — removing an Assignment, adding an Enrollment — and both land today, because nothing can state the rule (`FJS-2710` no arithmetic, `FJS-1305` no parent's column, `FJS-2778` a `@from` named in an expression is compiled as a column). **SRC's own daycare sample is under ratio on two shifts** (Monday and Tuesday toddler afternoons, one adult each for 8 and 5), and the rows agree with SRC's `ratioInfo` on all six. A roster is built in order — enroll the room, then staff it — and passes through *under ratio* at every step before the last.
  - **A** — refuse on both writes: `@minItems` takes an expression over the parent's columns (`assignments Assignment[] @minItems(ceil(event.enrolled * event.ratioAdults / event.ratioAttendees))`), graded at the outermost commit as `FJS-D347` grades a literal, and an Enrollment write re-grades every Shift of its Event. The price: SRC's sample cannot be loaded, a roster cannot be built one write at a time, and D347's *only parents a write touched* grows a second hop.
  - **B** — a flag the row carries: `underRatio Boolean @derived(adults * eventRatioAttendees < eventEnrolled * eventRatioAdults)` on Shift, the event's three values brought on by `FJS-D911`'s to-one `@from`. Every read says it, `where: { underRatio: true }` lists the rooms to fix, and SRC's badge is that field. No new word, once the three rows above close; it refuses nothing, and *answering for the room on the day* (a `@@commitment` at the shift's start, or a report) is a later question. **Owes its artefact, because B is wrong in silence exactly as `FJS-2778` measured** (a `@derived` over a `@from` reads one answer and filters another): `ratio.test.ts` flips to read `underRatio` off every shift and through `where: { underRatio: true }`, and asserts both agree with SRC's `ratioInfo`.
  - **C** — a validation warn: the write lands and the result carries a warning. Litestone has no warning channel on a write result, so this is a new mechanism, and it reaches whoever made the write rather than whoever answers for the room, and is gone by the next read.
  - **Recommend B** — strictness follows what a mistake destroys (`PHILOSOPHY.md` § IV): a plan under ratio harms nobody until the day, and refusing it at plan time makes a plan impossible to write — SRC's own sample is the evidence. B adds no word, closes three filed gaps that other apps already want (ELA's totals, linear's state category), and puts the rule where every surface reads it. If the owner wants a refusal, it belongs at the moment the shift starts, which is a commitment, not a commit.

- ~~**FJS-D989 — How does `@@exclude` grade a member's range read through a to-one relation, and regrade the children's keys when the parent's range moves?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-15 (`FJS-D989`): A — admit a to-one `@from(rel, column)` field (`FJS-D911`, built by `FJS-1305`, still open) as a range end: `startsAt DateTime @from(shift, startLocal)`, `range: [startsAt, endsAt]`. `gradeKey` reads the member's range through the relation's join, and the ledger gains a reverse edge: a write naming a column some member's range `@from`s notes every key the parent's children hold. `@from` stays the one owner of crossing a relation; waits on `FJS-1305`.** `FJS-2780`, eventmark's roster: Shift holds `startLocal`/`endLocal`, Assignment holds `personId`, many per Shift. `@@exclude(person, range: [shift.startLocal, shift.endLocal])` on Assignment is a token error (*Expected RBRACKET, got '.'*), and a `@from` field is refused as unstored (`parser.js` 5489). The copy an app writes instead (`startsAt`/`endsAt` on Assignment) is graded, but a probe moving the second Shift from 12:00 to 11:00 under one person's two Assignments returns no error where `OverlapConflictError` is owed: `createExclusionLedger.note` (`src/core/exclusion.js`) notes only a write to a CITING model, so a write to the parent grades nothing.
  - **A** — admit a to-one `@from(rel, column)` field (`FJS-D911`, built by `FJS-1305`, still open) as a range end: `startsAt DateTime @from(shift, startLocal)`, `range: [startsAt, endsAt]`. `gradeKey` reads the member's range through the relation's join, and the ledger gains a reverse edge: a write naming a column some member's range `@from`s notes every key the parent's children hold. `@from` stays the one owner of crossing a relation; waits on `FJS-1305`.
  - **B** — a one-hop dotted path inside `range:`: `range: [shift.startLocal, shift.endLocal]`, the path resolved as a policy's `relation.column` path is, with the same reverse edge. The spelling the author reaches for first, but it is the second way to cross a relation — the shape `FJS-D911` refused for `@derived` (its option C).
  - **C** — keep the copy and close the hole at the parent: a parse refusal naming the path rather than a token error, plus a documented hazard that a copied range goes stale on the parent's write. Smallest; the roster keeps a hand-kept copy that is silently wrong after a Shift moves.
  - **Recommend A** — `FJS-D911` already ruled `@from` the owner of bringing a parent's column onto a row, so the range is derived rather than copied and the only new mechanism is the reverse edge, which every option except C needs anyway.

- ~~**FJS-D1009 — When CI's structure phase grades a rule an app's `check-baseline.json` never adopted, is the debt adopted with a reasoned count or lowered to zero before CI goes green?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-15 (`FJS-D1009`): A — Adopt the brand-new rules with `fli check --adopt` and a `//` reason each (css-raw-literal 33, css-class-collision 5, example app-layout 7), and fix the two +1 regressions, since a count that moved is a new site rather than old debt. CI goes green today; each adopted key is a ceiling that falls as rows move them.** FJS-2371 made CI grade the ratchet, and `node scripts/ci.mjs --phase structure` now fails on two apps (FJS-2862). Basecamp's baseline has no `css-raw-literal` (33) or `css-class-collision` (5) key at all, and two adopted counts moved up one each: `detail-read-dead` 7/6 (`web/src/components/widgets/ServiceHealthBody.mesa:36` is among them) and `package-model-drift` 3/2 (the new one is `model User`'s `email` against `@frontierjs/auth`, whose `plugin.ts` is dirty in the tree). Example's baseline has no `app-layout` key (7, e.g. `web/src/displays.js`, `api/src/core/channels.ts`). Every existing adopted count in basecamp carries a `//` reason naming its sites.
  - **A** — Adopt the brand-new rules with `fli check --adopt` and a `//` reason each (css-raw-literal 33, css-class-collision 5, example app-layout 7), and fix the two +1 regressions, since a count that moved is a new site rather than old debt. CI goes green today; each adopted key is a ceiling that falls as rows move them.
  - **B** — Adopt nothing: fix all 45 sites (or `allow` the collisions that are deliberate, as example already does for its four) before the phase goes green. Leaves CI red for every session until done.
  - **C** — Adopt everything as it stands, including the two +1s, with reasons.
  - **Recommend A** — it matches how basecamp's `app-layout: 11` was adopted when FJS-1888 first graded that rule, and keeps a regression a regression; the +1s get their own rows.

- ~~**FJS-D1018 — How does a Basecamp monitor reach a private-network target once every probe is graded by junction's `assertPublicUrl`?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-15 (`FJS-D1018`): A — no escape hatch: every request and every redirect hop is graded, and a private target waits for the paper's Outpost `vantage` column ([uptime-monitoring.md](IDEAS/uptime-monitoring.md) § Design, *Vantage*), where the probe runs on a machine the operator put on that network. Cost: an install watching LAN hosts loses them until vantage ships.** `probe()` ([monitor-sweep.job.ts](packages/basecamp/api/src/jobs/monitor-sweep.job.ts)) hands `monitor.url` and every redirect `location` to `fetch` unchecked, and nothing in basecamp calls `@frontierjs/junction/public-url`, so a workspace admin (`Monitor` is `@@gate("2.5")`) points a monitor at `http://169.254.169.254/` or `http://localhost:5503/` and reads back the status code, latency and error text (`FJS-2879`). The red test is a sweep over a monitor at `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/`: today it records a `statusCode`, and the fix should record a refusal instead. Grading each hop with `assertPublicUrl(url, { allowHttp: true })` is settled by `FJS-681`/`FJS-1578` (one owner for the ranges). What is open is the escape hatch, because a self-hosted install legitimately monitors LAN hosts.
  - **A** — no escape hatch: every request and every redirect hop is graded, and a private target waits for the paper's Outpost `vantage` column ([uptime-monitoring.md](IDEAS/uptime-monitoring.md) § Design, *Vantage*), where the probe runs on a machine the operator put on that network. Cost: an install watching LAN hosts loses them until vantage ships.
  - **B** — A, plus one install-wide `allowPrivate` in Basecamp's config, written by the operator and never by a row, passed through as conduit's `destinations` already is. Cost: once it is on, loopback and the metadata service are open to every admin in every workspace.
  - **C** — A, plus a per-monitor `allowPrivate` column that only an operator level can write, so one named LAN monitor is opted in and every other monitor stays graded. Cost: a column and a field-level gate, and an opted-in name that re-resolves somewhere worse is not caught.
  - **Recommend A** — it adds no option and follows both the paper (private targets belong to an Outpost) and the guard's own default. An admin is a workspace role and not an operator, and B and C each give that role the reach the row is about.

- ~~**FJS-D1006 — How does an Oracle answer limit a move or a write to a membership role?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-15 (`FJS-D1006`): B for moves, A's `roles` for the other ops.** `checkAnswer` (`packages/oracle/src/answer.js:325`) accepts only `via`, `members`, `public`, `publicWhen`, `shared`, `system` and `why` on an entity's access, and `accessFor` (`packages/oracle/src/emit.js`) writes every `members` entity's update as `<list>.some(userId == auth().id)` with no role term, while `transitions()` writes a move with no `by` but `@system`. So Charter's Lago answer (Finance finalizes and voids invoices, Support changes nothing) has no spelling: every active member reaches every Invoice move and write. The red test: an emitted schema whose Membership has `role: admin|finance|support`, a `support` member's `invoice.finalize` and `invoice.update` expected refused; both succeed today. `FJS-D623` asks the same question for the container's admin only, so the two share a spelling or the answer carries two.
  - **A** — a `roles` key on the entity's access naming membership role values per operation, `roles: { update: ['finance'], delete: ['finance'] }`, and a move named by key (`roles: { finalize: ['finance'], void: ['finance'] }`); `checkAnswer` refuses a role the container's membership enum lacks, and `FJS-D623`'s `admins` becomes `roles: { update: [...] }` on the container, one key for both. A write the key omits keeps today's member reach.
  - **B** — a `by` on each lifecycle move (`finalize: open -> final by finance`) beside the existing `by: 'system'`, and a plain `writers: ['finance']` on access for non-move writes. Moves read where they are declared; two spellings for one idea, and `FJS-D623` stays a third.
  - **C** — a role is the product's, so Oracle emits none: the answer states it as an `open` item the emitter prints and the app writes its own gate. This is today's behavior, and Charter's People step stops collecting roles Oracle cannot honour.
  - **Recommend B for moves, A's `roles` for the other ops** — a move's role is stated where the move is declared, extending `by` (already `'system'`) rather than naming the move a second time in `access` as A does; a plain write has no move to carry it, so `roles: { update: [...] }` on access states it once, and `FJS-D623`'s `admins` is that key on the container. `checkAnswer` refuses a role the membership enum lacks, and an op or move whose role cannot resolve emits system-only, so a typo fails closed.

- ~~**FJS-D942 — Does FJS-2646 close by the load route (FJS-2645), with the git build's Dockerfile path left to FJS-2123, or does the git build learn to vendor?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-16 (`FJS-D942`): A — the image is built where the workspace is: `fli` vendors and builds (Kamal's model) and ships the image to the machine, and the release names its digest, which `pull` already returns without pulling when the machine holds it (FJS-2645, closed 2026-10-13). How the image reaches the machine (`docker save | ssh docker load`, or a load route) is the build's own step, owed. A git source's subdirectory is already its context (FJS-2123, closed); a `source.dockerfile` is added only for a repo with no out-of-repo dependency. FJS-2646 closes when the image path is built.** `docker.build` runs `docker build -t image dir` (`packages/outpost/src/docker.js`, `build`) with no `-f` and no context field, and a `fli new` app keeps its Dockerfile at `deploy/Dockerfile` and its `link:` dependencies in `deploy/generated/`, which only `fli deploy:vendor` on a machine holding the workspace can write. A red test that asserts `-f deploy/Dockerfile` passes the flag still leaves the build failing at `COPY deploy/generated`, so the fix has no honest small form until this is ruled.
  - **A** — the image is built where the workspace is: `fli` vendors and builds (Kamal's model) and ships the image to the machine, and the release names its digest, which `pull` already returns without pulling when the machine holds it (FJS-2645, closed 2026-10-13). How the image reaches the machine (`docker save | ssh docker load`, or a load route) is the build's own step, owed. A git source's subdirectory is already its context (FJS-2123, closed); a `source.dockerfile` is added only for a repo with no out-of-repo dependency. FJS-2646 closes when the image path is built.
  - **B** — the git build runs a declared prebuild step on the machine before `docker build`. Needs the workspace on the machine, and no PaaS does it.
  - **C** — `fli` uploads the vendored tree as an `inline` source and Outpost builds it with `-f`. Reuses the inline route but ships a source tarball per release, and builds on the machine what A builds where the workspace is.
  - **Recommend A** — the prior art (`IDEAS/paas-parity.md`, item 2) has every platform expecting a context that holds all of the app, so a `link:` dependency can only be built where it lives.

- ~~**FJS-D943 — Where do the bytes of a volume restore come from, and what stops it overwriting a live database?**~~ **Answered 2026-10-16 (`FJS-D943`): B first, A as the add-on.** `FJS-2648`: no Outpost route writes into `fjs-<app>-data` (`docker.js` mounts it at `-v ${name}-data:${config.volumePath}` on deploy and nothing else touches its contents), so adopting an app means `docker cp` by hand. Every Outpost route takes a JSON body (`server.js`, `POST /static/publish` carries `files` in it), which cannot carry ELA's 4.6 GB. The red test would assert: `POST /volume/restore` for a stopped app with an empty volume leaves the archive's files in the volume; today the route answers 404. Related: `FJS-D679` ruled A (2026-10-13), so a backup is an archive `POST /backup` leaves on the machine, and its restore is "stop → restore → start" from that path.
  - **A** — the machine fetches: body `{ app_id, url, sha256, overwrite? }`; Outpost streams the URL into `tar -x` inside a throwaway container mounting the volume, checks the digest, and returns file count and bytes. Nothing large crosses Basecamp or the signed command port (the prior art's shape, `paas-parity.md` item 4). Costs an outbound fetch of a URL a caller states, so the route must refuse non-https and private addresses, and `s3://` needs a credential on the machine (`fleet-ingress.md` D6 refused one for DNS).
  - **B** — a path already on the machine: body `{ app_id, path, overwrite? }`, the file placed there by `scp`/`rsync` or by a `FJS-D679` option-A backup. No network code in Outpost and no SSRF surface; the operator copies the 4.6 GB once, and a restore from a `D679` backup is the same call. Costs a manual step for adoption and a root the path must sit under, so a signed caller cannot name `/etc`.
  - **C** — stream the tar as the request body to `POST /volume/restore?app_id=…`: the one route here whose body is bytes, signed over a digest header. Works from anywhere with no URL or path, but the 4.6 GB passes through whatever holds the stream, and the signature scheme (`server.js` recomputes it from the raw URL and JSON body) needs a bytes variant.
  - **Either way** — the App must be stopped (a container holds the volume: refuse naming it) and a non-empty volume is refused unless `overwrite: true`, which `D679` need not settle.
  - **Recommend B first, A as the add-on** — B is the smallest piece that unblocks ELA and serves the `D679` restore, with no new attack surface; A adds one fetch step in front of the same extract, so it can follow without changing the route's other parameters.

## Shapes — from the 2026-10-06 cross-schema review (not yet filed; `fli file --sev decision` each before building on it)

Source: *The Ten Shapes* (https://claude.ai/artifact/KFxSqaTYsKfZLcVquhfQVt) and `packages/litestone/references/` as of 2026-10-06. Each is a question a reference file answered provisionally in its own prose; the file says which way it leaned.

- **The catalog word for the container people belong to.** Nine instances, four words (`Workspace`, `Account`, `Site`, `Organization`) and none of them Oracle's; `references/Organization.lite` takes `Organization` as the catalog word with the app's word as `@@label`.
  - **A** — `Organization`, as written: matches Oracle, trigger.dev, documenso; no FrontierJS app uses it.
  - **B** — `Workspace`: what basecamp, linear and notion say, and the word `tenancy { claim workspaceId }` already carries in two prototypes.
  - **C** — no catalog word: the shape is `Member`'s relator and the container is always the app's noun.
  - **Recommend A** — a catalog word that no app uses is the point: it names the shape without claiming the label, which is what `@@label` is for.

- **Whether the polymorphic subject's spelling is settled.** `references/README.md` chose `subjectType`/`subjectId`; eleven copies of the notifications fragment now say `contextType`/`contextId`, and every new app votes that way by scaffold.
  - **A** — settled as `subject*`; the fragment renames when a migration is cheap (pre-alpha: now).
  - **B** — settled as `context*`; AuditEvent and the README move to it.
  - **C** — leave both and have `fli check` grade only the enum-on-the-type-column half.
  - **Recommend A, now** — the evolution policy says a rename is a rename; the only cost is one fragment and one drift baseline, and it stops growing by one app a week.

- **Whether a trait may ship a `claim`.** `Grant` is unusable without `claim xId` + `@@allow(…, id == auth().xId)`, both the host's today because a trait cannot name the host's key.
  - **A** — no; a trait is columns and model attributes, the claim is a schema-level fact, the README says so and `grant.lite` in auth would ship the claim beside the trait.
  - **B** — yes, with a parameter: `@@trait(Grant, claim: bookingId)` expands the policy.
  - **Recommend A** — B is a language feature for one trait; the auth fragment is the shape `user.lite` already has.

- **Whether `Decision` is a trait or an attribute on the transition.** `references/Decision.lite` says it wants to be `approve: requested -> approved @stamps(decidedAt, decidedById)`.
  - **A** — trait now, attribute later; a host that spreads the trait renames nothing when the attribute arrives.
  - **B** — the attribute now, no trait: the stamp is owned by the edge that writes it, which is the one-owner invariant.
  - **Recommend A** — B is right and is litestone work with a catalog entry, a hook, and `x-transitions` to carry it; the trait costs nothing while that waits.

- **FJS-D945 — How is a relation count (litestone `include._count`, optionally filtered by a `where` on the target) spelled over the wire?** `parsePopulate` (`packages/junction/src/core/litestone.ts`, 440) spells `rel`, `rel:a+b` and dotted paths and never `_count`, and `$populate` is a CSV on the wire (`write: asCsv`, `packages/toolbelt/src/directives/directives.js`, 126), so a `where` has nowhere to sit. The red test's assertion: `GET /service-areas?$populate=_count.surveys` answers each row with `_count: { surveys: n }`, and a filtered one answers n over the matching surveys only — today the key is read as a relation named `_count` and the count is absent.
  - **A** — extend the `populate` grammar: `_count.surveys` unfiltered, and a filtered count spelled with the target's own filter keys, `_count.surveys?status=open`. Keeps one directive, but it is a mini-grammar inside a CSV, and a `where` in it needs its own escaping and `$`-key refusal (Invariants 8, 10).
  - **B** — a new directive row, `$count`, in the toolbelt table (`FJS-D125`'s one place): `$count=surveys` unfiltered, `$count={"surveys":{"where":{…}}}` structured with `write` as JSON. The filter reuses `parseWhere` for the target, so the `$`-key refusal and name checks have one owner. Costs a noun (§ V.2) and one more reserved key.
  - **C** — the object form of `populate` only, on bodies that can carry one (POST/WS `{ populate: { _count: { surveys: { where } } } }`), with no query-string spelling. Smallest, but forwards a caller-built include tree to litestone and the browser client's GET path cannot ask.
  - **Recommend B** — `where` is a structure and the directive table is where a per-call option with a structured form already lives; A restates `parseWhere` inside a CSV and C leaves the common GET unable to ask. The ELA case (one count per service area, filtered by date range) needs the filter, so the unfiltered-only half is not enough alone.

- **FJS-D963 — Does litestone DERIVE the instant columns a wall clock and its zone resolve to, and recompute them when either source changes?** `FJS-D512` took A and left B as its own ruling. Today `startsAt`/`endsAt` are app-written `@system` columns nothing marks as derived, so correcting `Site.timeZone` leaves every stored instant an hour out. Red assertion owed: update `site.timeZone` and a `startsAt` declared from `startLocal @zoned(site.timeZone)` reads the new instant.
  - **A** — `@zoned` on a `DateTime` names its wall-clock and zone sources; litestone writes it on create and recomputes it on a write to either source, and a zone write recomputes the children through the relation.
  - **B** — leave the pair app-owned; add a `db.<model>.rederive()` the app calls after a zone change. No new language, but the remembering stays with the app.
  - **C** — drop the stored instant; read it by a computed accessor from `@zoned` at read time. Nothing to go stale, but no index or `where` on the instant (`FJS-D908`).
  - **Recommend C** — it removes the copy that drifts rather than keeping it in sync, and `FJS-D908` already rules the per-zone lowering a `where` needs.

- **FJS-D964 — What spells a zone-less local datetime?** eventmark 1.3: a schedule whose times have no zone by design stores its `@@exclude` range as `String @datetime` with a `Z` it does not mean, since `@datetime` is an instant and `2025-08-25T09:00` is refused.
  - **A** — an option on the existing word, `@datetime(local: true)`, accepting `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM[:SS]` and refusing an offset; `@@exclude` grades it as text.
  - **B** — a new validator `@localdatetime` beside `@date`/`@time`: one more noun.
  - **C** — leave it; the app stores `@date` and `@time` as two columns. Keeps the vocabulary, but `@@exclude` cannot span the pair (`FJS-2781`).
  - **Recommend A** — it is the third kind `@date` and `@time` already complete, and an option on `@datetime` coins no noun.

- **FJS-D965 — `FJS-D679` widens `Backup` with an optional `appId` and drops `@@tenant(none)`, yet a row scoped through a parent that names no parent is `asSystem()`'s alone (`FJS-D481`, `FJS-D141`): how do the installation's own null-`appId` rows stay readable to the hub admin?** `FJS-1782`: `backups.service.ts` reads and writes through `db()` (the caller's membership claim) behind `requireSystemAdmin()`. Under the row desugar a `Backup` with no `appId`/`workspaceId` is denied to every tenant principal, so `/backups` stops listing the installation's own archives, and the ruling's "the installation's rows stay admin-only by row policy" has no policy to be written in: the desugar adds `@@deny`, never `@@allow`. Red test would assert: after the schema change, `db().backup.findMany()` as a sysadmin still returns a null-`appId` row AND an app-scoped row, and a member of another workspace sees neither.
  - **A** — keep `@@tenant(none)`, add nullable `workspaceId` and `appId` columns, and write the row policy by hand (`@@allow('read', workspaceId == auth().workspaceId)` for app rows; the installation's rows stay at the gate's system-admin level). One model and one list as D679 ruled, but it reverts to the hand-written allow the schema header says replaced sixteen of them, and a missed `workspaceId` stamp leaks an app's archive across workspaces.
  - **B** — coin a sibling `AppBackup` scoped through `App` by the ordinary desugar, leaving `Backup` the installation's own `@@tenant(none)` noun untouched. The tenant boundary is the schema's own and `backups.service.ts` needs no change, at the cost of two nouns and two lists where D679 chose one (the option it left open and then closed).
  - **C** — as D679 rules (`appId` only, no `@@tenant(none)`), and the hub's `/backups` reads the installation's rows through `asSystem()` behind `requireSystemAdmin()`. One noun, no hand-written allow, but `requireSystemAdmin()` becomes the only thing between a tenant and the installation's archives, which Invariant 6 puts in the schema.
  - **Recommend B** — the tenant boundary stays declared where Invariant 6 puts it, and the two lists D679 wanted merged are a screen's concern, not a noun's.

- **FJS-D973 — Does a plugin `ready()` run before the listener binds and fail `start()` on a throw, or stay after `listen` and forgiving?** `runStartPhases` runs `listen` then `ready-hooks` (`packages/junction/src/core/app.ts`), and a throw in `ready` is `logger.error`'d while the app goes on; `docs/internals.md` § Plugin phases states "only `ready` is forgiving" as the rule. The only shipped `ready()` is devtools, which binds its own admin port there and catches its own `EADDRINUSE` (`FJS-420`). Red test, FJS-2825: a plugin whose `ready()` throws — `start()` resolves and the app answers `/health`, where a plugin whose `boot()` throws rejects `start()`.
  - **A** — keep the order and the forgiveness, and say so: `ready` is "after the app is serving", for work that must not hold the first request (devtools' port). Closes FJS-2825 as documented behavior and fixes the `CLAUDE.md` line "only a throw in `ready` lets the app start", which reads as the opposite.
  - **B** — keep `ready` after `listen`, but a throw fails `start()` (shutdown unwinds plugins as `boot-plugins` does). The app can still answer requests before another plugin's `ready` finishes, and a failed `start()` leaves a bound port to release.
  - **C** — move `ready-hooks` before `listen` and make a throw fail `start()`, so nothing is answered before every plugin is ready. Devtools' `ready()` binds its own port and does not need `app.http.port`, but any third-party `ready()` reading `app.http.port` breaks (it is unbound until `listen`), and `needsHost` for the phase must still skip it in `_startForTest()`.
  - **Recommend A** — the only caller wants forgiveness (a held devtools port must not stop the app), so B and C buy strictness nothing asks for; work that must finish before the first request already has `boot()`.

- **FJS-D974 — How does a connection get into a bound room (`channels({ rooms })`) whose row was created after its socket opened: the client asks by `subscribe`, or the server joins every live connection the new row admits?** `FJS-2807`: the only server moment that names a connection is `channels.on('connection')`, and a `subscribe` from a non-member is ignored (`transport/channels.ts`, `parsed.type === 'subscribe'`), so a `SupportThread` created after the customer's socket opened answers no `presence:sync` on `support:{id}` until the socket reconnects. The red test's assertion: open a socket, create the row, send `subscribe` on `support:{id}`, expect a `presence:sync` listing that connection.
  - **A** — a `subscribe` on a BOUND name joins, through the same `admitToRoom` the server's join takes (graded at the Data boundary, re-asked after every write); an unbound name is ignored as today. No new option and no new noun; it amends `FJS-D175`'s "subscribe does NOT join" for exactly the names `FJS-D596` made gradable. Only the tab that asks is in the room, and each ask costs one row read, bounded by the frame rate.
  - **B** — a rule on `rooms`: when a write creates a row of a bound model, grade every live connection against it and join the admitted ones. Needs no client change, but it is an option or a rule word, grades O(connections) per create, and puts every tab of every admitted person in a room none of them opened, so presence lies about who is looking.
  - **C** — keep admission the app's: document the `localConnectionsOf(userId)` scan from a service hook as the paved road. Nothing to build, but it over-joins every tab of the session and each app restates the read policy in a hook (Invariant 6).
  - **Recommend A** — membership stays server-graded by the one owner, the client only asks to be considered, and presence shows who opened the room.

- **FJS-D975 — What word says a `@@transitions` move stamps a time column in the same write?** `FJS-2801`: `transition()` writes the target field alone (`client.js` `transition`, `data: { [targetField]: targetValue }`), and a `@@allow('update', … && status == 'draft')` hides the row from any update made after the move, so `sentAt` is stamped before the move (two writes for one event) or never. Red test's assertion: a model with `sentAt DateTime?`, `send: draft -> sent` and an update policy keyed on `status == 'draft'`; `transition(id, 'send')` then reads back `sentAt` non-null in the same audited write.
  - **A** — a per-move attribute beside `@gate`/`@system`/`@seals`: `send: draft -> sent @stamps(sentAt)`. `transition()` (and `update()` landing that move) puts the clock in the named `DateTime` columns in the one UPDATE, the way `@updatedAt` stamps; the column is engine-owned (a payload naming it is refused like `@system`). Move-keyed, so two moves into one state can stamp different columns, and it extends an attribute list that already exists.
  - **B** — a field attribute naming the state it records: `sentAt DateTime? @enteredAt(status.sent)`. The column declares itself, so every write that lands `status` in `sent` stamps it, `transition()` or a form's `update()` alike, and the row says where its time came from. A new noun on the field, and a state reached by two moves stamps once per entry unless it says otherwise.
  - **C** — neither: let `transition(id, name, { data })` carry extra columns written with the move. No new schema word, but the caller supplies the time (forgeable, restated per call site) and the stamp stays optional, so a direct `transition()` still leaves `sentAt` null.
  - **Recommend A** — the move is already the unit that carries `@gate`, `@system` and `@seals`, so the stamp belongs on it and the engine writes it, which is what makes a direct `transition()` unable to leave it null.

- **FJS-D980 — What spells a merge-field hole in text a user wrote, and is the body's format stated by the caller or sniffed?** `FJS-2680`: toolbelt has no `fill` (`rg 'fill\('` over `packages/toolbelt/src` is empty), orion's `data.template` is `{{x}}` with `String(val)` (`packages/orion/src/engine/nodes/index.ts:108`), and the apps that filled by hand wrote `{group.field}` (ela, ss-planner) and `{{expr}}` (orion, chatwoot). The red test is `fill('<p>{client.name}</p>', { client: { name: '<img onerror=x>' } }, { mode: 'html' })` returning `&lt;img…`, and `{ mode: 'text' }` returning it plain; no function exists to run it against.
  - **A** — `{path}` single brace, the path a dotted field name or a `toolbelt/predicate` values expression (`FJS-1998`). Matches both ela and ss-planner and what office users already type, and `fill` runs only on stored text, never `.mesa` source, so Mesa's `{…}` (`FJS-873`) never meets it. `data.template` moves onto `fill` in the same change, `{{x}}` gone, so orion keeps one spelling. A literal brace is written `\{`.
  - **B** — `{{path}}` double brace, the orion/chatwoot/Mustache spelling. Survives a sentence with a stray `{`, and cannot be mistaken for Mesa's `{…}`, but it moves ela and ss-planner's stored templates, and an office user types it less readily.
  - **C** — `fill` takes the hole delimiters as an argument and both spellings ship. Nothing moves, but it is an option, and two spellings for one thing is the state this row complains of.
  - **Format** — under any of A–C the caller states `mode: 'html' | 'text'` (escape the value in `html`, plain in `text`; `{ html, text }` for app-built markup), and `fill` never guesses from tags, which is what makes ela's `htmlOf` wrong. An unknown name is refused by a `check(source, fields)` beside `fill` (the registry is `FJS-2683`), a falsy value fills as written, and a field the context lacks is left as written.
  - **Recommend A** — it is the spelling both ported apps already stored in their templates, so no stored body changes, and the `data.template` move leaves one hole syntax in the repo rather than two.

- **FJS-D999 — What counts the distinct local days an instant column falls on, when SQLite has no zone database?** `FJS-2615`: `aggregate({ _count: { distinct: 'sentAt' } })` is `COUNT(DISTINCT col)` (`client.js` `aggregate`), so it counts instants, not days; ELA's contact bars key each instant through `Intl.DateTimeFormat` in a hand-written read step. Neither engine can do it in SQL exactly: `bun:sqlite` registers no user function (`rg 'createFunction' packages/litestone/src` is empty), and SQLite's date modifiers take a fixed offset, so `date(col, '-5 hours')` is wrong for the half of the year a DST zone is not on that offset. The zone is also the account's, a relation, which `_count` cannot read.
  - **A** — `_count: { distinct: { day: 'sentAt', offset: '-05:00' } }` (or a bound `$offset`): `COUNT(DISTINCT date(col, ?))`. One query, no new noun beyond the object form, but a fixed offset is the zone only outside DST, so the answer is silently an hour off across a transition — the `FJS-2170` failure again.
  - **B** — the app passes a day table: litestone grows `distinctDays(field, { zone, where })` as a helper that selects the instants (`select: { sentAt }`, graded and policy-filtered as any read) and keys them in JS through `Intl.DateTimeFormat`, DST-exact on either engine. A second read path beside `aggregate`, and O(rows) rather than one aggregate, but the rows are one parent's last 30 days.
  - **C** — nothing in litestone: this is a display-side question of the same family as `FJS-2008`, `FJS-D288` already ruled a zone is spent only where an instant and a day cross, and the toolbelt `datetime` kit is where that crossing lives (`toolbelt` grows `distinctDays(instants, zone)`). The read step stays, but calls one tested function instead of its own `Intl` keying. Closes `FJS-2615` as a toolbelt row.
  - **Recommend C** — no SQL form is exact (A) and a second read path in litestone (B) re-owns a crossing `FJS-D288` put in the kit; the caller already holds the instants, so one tested kit function removes the duplication with no new litestone surface.

- **FJS-D1000 — Should the theme switch offer a mode+accent pair, or stay one whole theme per choice?** `FJS-2513`: `applyTheme` (`packages/sierra/src/theme/index.js:266`) does `classList.add(value)` for one name from `theme.themes`, so `theme-dark` + `theme-sunset` (accent-only, `packages/css/src/themes/sunset.css`, which nests over any surface) cannot both come from the switch; `classList.add('theme-dark theme-sunset')` throws `InvalidCharacterError`. The red test: `setTheme('theme-dark theme-sunset')` with that entry declared leaves `<html>` carrying both classes and neither stale one; today it is refused or throws.
  - **A** — a `themes` entry may name several classes, space-separated. `applyTheme` splits it and removes every class of every declared entry before adding; `setTheme`, `toggleTheme`, persistence and `theme.value` keep treating the entry as one opaque choice, and `buildThemeScript` splits the same way. No new noun or option; a picker's swatch wears the entry's classes.
  - **B** — two axes: `theme: { modes, accents }` with `setMode` / `setAccent`, persisted under two keys and written as two classes. The honest model of "dark with a red accent", but two public verbs, a second persisted key and a `system` map that must say which accent follows the OS.
  - **C** — no switch change: the kit ships (or the app writes) one whole dark-with-accent theme per accent. Sierra is untouched, but it multiplies kit themes by modes and is the app stylesheet restating `theme-dark`'s tokens the row names as the cost.
  - **Recommend A** — the switch already owns a list of whole choices and the css themes already nest, so letting an entry name more than one class closes the gap without a second axis, a new verb or a second persisted key.
